Pressing the key again moves the active item down (Ctrl+Tab) or up (Ctrl+Shift+Tab) and goes round at the ends: with the picker open the same commands move (a key pressed twice before the input has the focus works too), and `quickOpenNavigateNextInEditorPicker` and `quickOpenNavigatePreviousInEditorPicker` do it where the keys resolve in the picker (`inQuickOpen && inEditorsPicker`, weight 250). A command without a key of one press (it was removed or run from the palette) opens a normal picker, with the input, with the same active item. Preview of the active item while moving is not done (VS Code does not do it either). The modifier keys of Enter and of a click are ignored while the picker is a switcher.
- **The choice on release.** `Controller.QuickNavigate` holds the modifiers of the chords of the keybindings of the command that opened it. `Host` adds a handler to the window's `HardwareKeyboard` (it sees every key before the focus does, wherever the focus is): the release of the modifier of one of the chords chooses the active row and ends the waiting; the release of Shift counts only while no other modifier is held (Ctrl+Shift+Tab lets Shift go first to go back); the wait is only for the first release. **When the release is missed** (it went to another window, or the focus was elsewhere): a key other than a modifier pressed while the keys are not held ends the waiting; the window losing the keyboard focus (`DidChangeViewFocus`) ends it too and closes the picker when `workbench.quickOpen.closeOnFocusLost` is on (default); and a click outside or the focus leaving the widget closes it as for any picker. After the waiting ends the picker is a normal one: Enter or a click chooses, Escape closes, so it is never stuck. Nothing here works around Glutter: key up events reach `HardwareKeyboard`'s handlers and `DidChangeViewFocus` tells when the window loses the keyboard; the tests send both through Glutter's test dispatcher, and the real keyboard of Wayland and X11 is a manual check (`docs/MANUAL-CHECKS.md`).
- **Not built.** Ctrl+P pressed again inside the file picker moving down (`quickOpenNavigateNextInFilePicker`, `quickOpenNavigatePreviousInFilePicker`), the open in background of an item (`canAcceptInBackground`), and the editor name of a side by side editor.

### 10.7 Notifications and dialogs

Notifications (`core/notify`) have a severity, message, actions, an optional progress, and a source. Any part of Emetos can post one from the start (before the UI shows them, they go to the log). Toasts show at the bottom right, at most 3 at once; the notification center keeps them; do-not-disturb hides non-error toasts. Modal dialogs (unsaved changes, overwrite, delete) are in-window (`kit.Dialog`), keyboard operable.

**Notifications as built (step 6.11).** The model (`core/notify`, port of `NotificationsModel` and `NotificationViewItem`): a `Notification` has a `Severity` (`Info`, `Warning`, `Error`), a `Message`, an optional `Source`, `Actions` (the buttons: `Label`, `Command` and `Args`, a `Run` callback and `KeepOpen`; the first is the primary button, the others are secondary buttons), `Sticky`, `Silent` and an optional `Progress` (`Infinite`, or `Total` and `Worked`, and `Done`; a progress is running while it is not done and is infinite or has a total or some work done). `Service.Notify` returns the id (0 without a message) and logs the notification as before; a notification equal to one in the list (same severity, message, source and button labels, neither with a progress) replaces it. `Service` also has `Get`, `Items` (oldest first), `Dismiss`, `ClearAll` (newest first), `UpdateProgress`, `SetExpanded` and `SetDoNotDisturb`/`DoNotDisturb`, and tells its listeners with `Added`, `Dismissed`, `Updated` and `DoNotDisturbChanged` events (from any goroutine). An item with buttons starts expanded and cannot be collapsed; an item is sticky when `Sticky` is set, when it is an error with buttons, when it was expanded (and has no buttons) or when its progress is running (`Item.IsSticky`). Do not disturb (`notifications.toggleDoNotDisturbMode`; VS Code's `NotificationsFilter.ERROR`) makes the notifications that are not errors silent when they are sent, and hides the toasts that show when it is turned on; it is not a setting and does not survive the process.

`notify.Presenter` (one per window, `Window.Notifications`; port of the state of `NotificationsToasts` and `NotificationsCenter`) lives on the UI thread and decides what shows. `Bind(post, after)` makes it follow the service (the events are handed to the UI thread with `post`, the timers are `after`, the timer of the UI thread) and makes toasts of the notifications that are in the service already. A toast is made for an added notification unless the center is open, the item is silent, or more than `SpamLimit` (3) toasts were made within `SpamInterval` (800 ms) of the first. `Toasts()` lists the toasts that show, newest first, at most `MaxToasts` (3): the newest three; an older one comes back when a newer one goes away. A toast goes away after `PurgeTimeout` (10 s for an information, 12 s for a warning, 15 s for an error; VS Code's values), unless its item is sticky, the pointer is over it or the focus is in it, in which case the time starts again; the notification stays in the center. Opening the center (`ShowCenter`) takes the toasts away, and no toast shows while it is open; `ClearAll` closes the center and dismisses everything.

The widgets (`gui/workbench/notifications*.go`). `NotificationsHost` is placed once at the root of the workbench, over the workbench and below the dialogs (`workbench.Options.Notifications`), and reads the presenter. Toasts stack at the bottom right above the status bar (3 px from the window and 3 px above the status bar, with 4 px of margin around each toast, so the box is 7 px from both), 450 px wide at most (the window minus 16 px), the newest on top, each on `notifications.background` with `notifications.foreground`, a 1 px `notificationToast.border` border (none when transparent), a radius of 4 and a shadow. A row (`notificationRow`, the same in the toasts and in the center) is padded 10 px by 5 px: the severity icon (a Codicon `info`, `warning` or `error` at 18 px, in `notificationsInfoIcon.foreground`, `notificationsWarningIcon.foreground` or `notificationsErrorIcon.foreground`), the message on 22 px lines (13 px; one line with an ellipsis, or all of it when the item is expanded), and a toolbar that shows while the pointer is over the row, the focus is in it or the item is expanded: the expand (chevron up) or collapse (chevron down) button, shown when the item can collapse and is expanded, has a source, or its message does not fit on one line, and the close button ("Clear Notification"; none while a progress runs). An expanded item with a source or buttons has a details row (34 px, 8 px below the message): "Source: <source>" (12 px) and the buttons at the right (`kit.Button`, the first primary and the others secondary); a button runs its `Run`, then its `Command` (an error is reported as an error notification "Cannot run <command>: ..."), and then closes the notification unless `KeepOpen`. The progress shows as a `kit.ProgressBar` along the bottom of the row. The notification center is the same place (7 px from the window and from the status bar), 450 px wide at most, with a 1 px `notificationCenter.border` border, a radius of 4 and a shadow: a header of 35 px (`notificationCenterHeader.background`, `notificationCenterHeader.foreground` or the workbench's text) with the title "NOTIFICATIONS" (11 px, "NO NEW NOTIFICATIONS" when there is none), and the buttons Clear All Notifications (disabled with nothing to clear: items with a running progress are not cleared by it), Enable or Disable Do Not Disturb Mode (a toggle, `bell-slash`) and Hide Notification Center (chevron down), over the list of all the notifications, newest first, separated by 1 px lines in `notifications.border`, at most 400 px high (and no more than the window leaves) and scrolling with a `kit.ScrollableElement`. Opening the center gives it the focus (and closing it gives the focus back to what had it); the pointer and the focus pause the toasts. There is no animation: toasts appear and go away at once (VS Code slides and fades them in 300 ms).

Colors registered (VS Code's defaults): `notificationCenter.border`, `notificationToast.border` (`widget.border`; `contrastBorder` in high contrast), `notifications.foreground` (`editorWidget.foreground`), `notifications.background` (`editorWidget.background`), `notificationLink.foreground` (`textLink.foreground`, which is registered too: #3794FF, #006AB1, #21A6FF, #0F4A85; links in messages are not built), `notificationCenterHeader.foreground` (none), `notificationCenterHeader.background` (`notifications.background` lightened by 0.3 in dark themes and darkened by 0.05 in light ones), `notifications.border` (the header background), `notificationsErrorIcon.foreground`, `notificationsWarningIcon.foreground` and `notificationsInfoIcon.foreground` (`editorError.foreground`, `editorWarning.foreground`, `editorInfo.foreground`).

Commands (`core/command/builtin.go`; the host binds the handlers while it is on screen): `notifications.showList` (Show Notifications, Ctrl+K Ctrl+Shift+N), `notifications.hideList` (Hide Notifications, Escape when `notificationCenterVisible`, weight `WorkbenchContrib+50`), `notifications.toggleList`, `notifications.hideToasts` (Escape when `notificationToastsVisible`, weight `WorkbenchContrib-50`, and `WorkbenchContrib+100` when `notificationFocus` too), `notifications.focusToasts` (Focus Notification Toast: the newest toast takes the focus), `notifications.clearAll` (Clear All Notifications) and `notifications.toggleDoNotDisturbMode` (Toggle Do Not Disturb Mode); the palette titles have the category "Notifications". The host sets the context keys `notificationCenterVisible`, `notificationToastsVisible` and `notificationFocus` (the focus is in a toast or in the center) in the scope of the window. VS Code has no menu row for them. Its bell in the status bar is built (step 6.20, §10.1 "Status bar as built"): `workbench.NewBell` follows the presenter, counts the unread notifications and runs `notifications.showList` and `notifications.hideList`. Not built: `notification.clear`, `notification.expand`, `notification.collapse` and `notification.toggle` for the notification that has the focus, `notifications.focusNextToast` and its kin, `notifications.toggleDoNotDisturbModeBySource` (it needs the quick input, step 6.17) and the other do-not-disturb filters by source, `notification.acceptPrimaryAction`, the secondary actions and the configure menu of an item, links and markdown in messages, the position settings (`workbench.notifications.position`), the rule that hides toasts that do not fit in the height of the window, the wait for the window to have the focus before a toast goes away, and the middle click that closes a notification.

**Dialogs** (`kit/dialog.go`, step 6.5; port of `dialog.ts` and `dialog.css`). A `DialogSpec` has a `Type` (`DialogInfo`, `DialogWarning`, `DialogError`, `DialogQuestion` (the information icon, as VS Code), `DialogNone`), a `Message` and a `Detail` (with a detail the message is a bold 14 px title over the 13 px detail on 20 px lines, whose line breaks are kept; without one the message is shown as the detail), `Buttons` (labels, "OK" by default; the marks of a mnemonic `&&` are left out), a `CancelButton` index, `DisableClose`, an optional checkbox (`CheckboxLabel`, `CheckboxChecked`) and `OnAnswer(DialogResult{Button, Checked, Cancelled})`. Button 0 is the default: primary (the others are secondary), focused when the dialog opens. The buttons show in Linux order (`DialogButtonOrder`, from `rearrangeButtons`): the cancel button right before the default one, the default one last. `Escape`, the close button (a 16 px "x" at the top right, left out with `DisableClose`) and `DialogController.Dismiss` cancel: `Button` is `CancelButton` (-1 when there is none) and `Cancelled` is true; a button answers with its index. The box is 480 px wide (at most 90 % of the window, 90 % of its height, the detail scrolling past that), padded 8 px, with a radius of 12, a 1 px `widget.border` border and a `widget.shadow` shadow (8 px blur) on `editorWidget.background`, text in `editorWidget.foreground`; the 24 px type icon is in `problemsErrorIcon.foreground`, `problemsWarningIcon.foreground` or `problemsInfoIcon.foreground` (VS Code has no `dialog.*` colors: its dialog styles are those, `editorWidget.*`, `widget.border` and `widget.shadow`). Keys: Tab and Shift+Tab (and Right and Left) go round the checkbox and the buttons (never out of the dialog), Enter and Space press the focused button, Enter and Space flip the focused checkbox (VS Code's `Toggle`), Escape cancels, and Enter with the focus on the dialog itself presses the default button. Not ported: inputs, the vertical alignment, custom bodies and footers, links, the button with a dropdown, the pending type and the mnemonics.

`DialogHost` (`NewDialogHost(child, controller)`) is placed once at the root of the workbench: it shows the dialog of a `DialogController` over its child in the same stack, in the middle of a scrim of 50 % black. The dialog is modal: the child keeps its place in the tree, so its state, but its pointer events are absorbed and its focus is excluded (the scrim also swallows the clicks, and a click on it brings the focus back into the dialog); the focus the window had when the first dialog opened is given back when the last one closes (if that widget is still there). `DialogController.Show(spec)` adds a dialog: one shows at a time, the others wait in order (`Pending`), a dialog shown from an `OnAnswer` goes behind the ones that wait; `IsShowing` tells the keybinding dispatcher of the window to let no command through, `Current`, `Dismiss` and `Clear` (drops the waiting ones) complete it. The controller is used from the UI thread only; other goroutines use the binding's `Post`.

### 10.8 Icons for files

VS Code's file icon themes are a font plus a JSON mapping (file extensions, file names, language ids and folder names to font characters and colors, with light-theme variants). Emetos bundles Seti, VS Code's default (`extensions/theme-seti/icons/seti.woff` and `vs-seti-icon-theme.json`), loads its font through `RunAppOpts.Fonts`, and reads the mapping in VS Code's icon theme format, so the explorer, tabs, breadcrumbs and quick open show the same icons as VS Code. "None" (no file icons) is the other choice.

**As built (step 6.14).**

*File icon themes* (`core/theme/fileicons.go`; port of `processIconThemeDocument` of `fileIconThemeData.ts` and of `getIconClasses.ts`; no Glutter import). `ParseFileIconTheme(id, data)` reads a theme file in VS Code's format (`iconDefinitions` with `fontCharacter`, `fontColor`, `fontSize` and `fontId`; `fonts`; `file`, `folder`, `folderExpanded`, `rootFolder`, `rootFolderExpanded`; `fileExtensions`, `fileNames`, `languageIds`, `folderNames`, `folderNamesExpanded`, `rootFolderNames`, `rootFolderNamesExpanded`; and the `light` and `highContrast` sections; a definition with an image path is not read) and fails with `FileIconThemeInvalid`; `BundledFileIconTheme("vs-seti")` is the Seti theme (`FileIconThemeNotFound` for another id). `IconFor(FileIconQuery{Kind, Name, Parent, LanguageID, Expanded, Type})` gives the `FileIcon` (the glyph, the family of its font, the color when the theme has one, the size as a factor of the text: 1.5 for Seti's "150%") or false. VS Code makes a CSS rule of each association and lets the specificity of the selectors choose, so the choice is by the number of class selectors of the rule: the default file icon 2, a language 3, an extension `segments + 3` (an extension is matched by a dot before it in the lowercase name, so `a.test.go` has `test.go` and `go`, and `test.go` beats `go`), a file name `segments + 4`, a key written `folder/name` one more when the folder the file is in has that name; for a folder the default 2, a name 3, the "expanded" rules 4 more when the folder is open (so an open folder takes `folderNamesExpanded`, then `folderExpanded`, then `folderNames`); the section of the theme type (`light` for light themes, `highContrast` for both high contrast themes) adds 1 to its rules, so it beats the document's own rules of the same kind; a tie goes to the file name, then the extension, then the language. `jsonc` has the icon of `json` when it has none of its own. A definition that has no character draws nothing and is skipped. Seti has no folder icons, as in VS Code: folders show their chevron and name only. The mapping is `internal/core/theme/iconassets/vs-seti-icon-theme.json`, copied by `scripts/update-assets.sh` (its license is `third_party/seti/`, listed in `NOTICE`); the font is the one of step 0.4. The setting `workbench.iconTheme` (default `vs-seti`, `null` for none) chooses the theme; `Window.FileIcons()` loads it (an unknown id is reported once as a warning and shows no icons). `kit.Colors.Type` is the type of the color theme. `explorer.FileIconWidget` draws the glyph in the family of the theme at 13 px times the scale (19.5 px for Seti), in the color of the theme or the color of the text, centered in a box 16 px wide and 22 px high, with 6 px between it and the label. The icons are used by the Explorer and the Open Editors; the tabs, the breadcrumbs, the Problems and the quick open still show the Codicons they had.

*The Explorer container* (`workbench.view.explorer`, "Show Explorer", Ctrl+Shift+E shows it and focuses the tree). It has the views of §10.4: **Open Editors** (above, hidden until it is shown, as the descriptor of VS Code 1.143 says: `workbench.files.action.focusOpenEditorsView`, Ctrl+K E, shows it, unfolds it and focuses its list; the container's own menu that shows and hides views is not built, `View.HideByDefault` is the switch), then either **Folders** (the tree of the folder; its header has the name of the folder, as VS Code's, or "Folders" with none) or, in a window with no folder, **No Folder Opened** with the welcome content.

*The folder view* (`gui/views/explorer`, `View`, `NewBody`, `NewActions`; port of `explorerView.ts` and of the data source and renderer of `explorerViewer.ts`). A `kit.Tree` keyed by path over a `workspace.Workspace`, showing the children of the folder at the top (the folder itself is the header). A row is the twistie, the icon of the file (the language comes from the registry, as a document's does, so `main.go` has the Go icon and `Makefile` the make one) and the name; a folder with `explorer.compactFolders` shows a chain of single child folders as one row, `a/b/c`, keyed by the first folder (`workspace.LoadedChain` gives the chain of what was read; reading a folder also reads the chain of its only child, as VS Code resolves single child descendants). Folders are read lazily in the background with the context of the window (the row shows "Loading..." meanwhile); a folder that cannot be read stays closed, so the next expansion tries again, and is reported as an error notification; a folder of the window that cannot be read shows its error in place of the tree. The rows are made again, once for any number of changes, when `Workspace.OnChange` fires (everything but a new size or time), and the expansion, the focus and the selection stay (they are by key). **Mouse and keys** are the tree's (§10.2): a click on a file selects it and opens it as a preview editor in the active group (`workbench.editor.enablePreview`), a double click or Enter keeps it; a click on a folder opens or closes it; Right, Left, Up, Down, Home, End and the page keys move as the tree's; a letter starts the find box (highlight mode, a fuzzy match). The keyboard focus stays in the tree when a preview opens. **Reveal** (`View.Reveal(path, RevealSelect | RevealNoScroll, done)`): the folders above the path are read in the background, then the rows are made again, the folders above the node are opened, the node is selected and focused, and (not for `RevealNoScroll`) scrolled into view after the next frame; a path outside the folder, one that `files.exclude` hides, or one that is not there is not found. `explorer.autoReveal` (`true`, `false`, `"focusNoScroll"`) reveals the file of the active editor when it changes and when the folder view comes on screen (the side bar shows the Explorer and the view is not folded). Not ported: `explorer.autoRevealExclude`, the find provider of the Explorer (the tree's own find box is used), the progress bar of a slow read, the decorations (git, problems) and the compact folder navigation commands. The buttons in the header of the folder view (shown while the pointer is over the view or the focus is in it): Refresh Explorer (`workbench.files.action.refreshFilesExplorer`: reads the folders that were read again) and Collapse Folders in Explorer (`workbench.files.action.collapseExplorerFolders`); New File and New Folder start the inline input (step 6.15, below).

*Open Editors* (`OpenEditors`, `NewOpenEditorsBody`; port of `openEditorsView.ts`): a `kit.List` of the editors of every group in grid order, with a row for each group ("GROUP 1", bold, 11 px) when there are several groups. An editor row is the close button, the icon, the name (in italics for the preview editor) and the folder dimmed; the button is a dot while the editor has unsaved changes, a cross for the row under the pointer, the selected row and a dirty editor (a click on it closes the editor). The active editor of the active group is selected. A click on a row makes its editor the active one of its group and the group the active group, a double click or Enter keeps it; a click on a group makes it active. `explorer.openEditors.sortOrder` (`editorOrder`, `alphabetical`, `fullPath`; the sorts of `getElements`) orders each group, `explorer.openEditors.visible` (default 9) is the number of rows the body has room for before it scrolls (at least one, at most the number of rows). Not built: the count of unsaved editors in the header, the drag and drop of editors, the context menu, `explorer.openEditors.minVisible` and the middle click.

*Welcome* (`NewWelcome`): "You have not yet opened a folder." over a button "Open Folder" (and, with editors open, "Opening a folder will close all currently open editors."). The button runs `workbench.action.files.openFolder` ("Open Folder...", Ctrl+K Ctrl+O, File menu row in group `2_open`), which shows the folder dialog of the desktop (the file chooser portal through `desktop.OpenFiles` with `Directory`, on a goroutine; a dialog that fails is the error `OpenFolderDialogFailed` and an error notification; a cancel does nothing) and opens the folder chosen with `Window.SetFolder` (§10.11). `workbench.action.closeFolder` ("Close Workspace", Ctrl+K F, File menu row "Close Folder" while a folder is open) gives the window no folder.

*Commands* (`core/command/builtin.go`, bound by `gui/app/explorer.go`; the view-less ones are no-ops): `workbench.view.explorer`, `workbench.explorer.fileView.focus` and `workbench.files.action.focusFilesExplorer` (show the Explorer and focus the tree), `workbench.files.action.focusOpenEditorsView`, `workbench.files.action.showActiveFileInExplorer` ("Reveal Active File in Explorer View"), `revealInExplorer` ("Reveal in Explorer View": the argument is a path or the context of a tab, else the active editor; it shows the Explorer, reveals the file and focuses the tree; a file outside the folder goes to the Open Editors when they are shown; the row is in the context menu of a tab), `workbench.files.action.refreshFilesExplorer`, `workbench.files.action.collapseExplorerFolders`, `workbench.action.files.openFolder` and `workbench.action.closeFolder`. **Context keys** on the window's keys: `workbenchState` (`"folder"` or `"empty"`), `explorerViewletVisible`, `foldersViewVisible`, `explorerViewletFocus`, `filesExplorerFocus`, `openEditorsFocus` (the focus is in the view, its header included), `explorerResourceIsFolder` (the focused row), `explorerResourceIsRoot` and `explorerResourceReadonly` (always false: there is no root row and no read-only folder) and `groupFocusedInOpenEditors`. **Settings**: `explorer.autoReveal`, `explorer.openEditors.visible`, `explorer.openEditors.sortOrder` and `workbench.iconTheme`.

**The file icon theme picker as built (step 8.4; `core/theme/pick.go`, `gui/app/themepickers.go`).** `File Icon Theme` (`workbench.action.selectIconTheme`, category Preferences, no key) opens a quick pick "Select File Icon Theme (Up/Down Keys to Preview)" under the separator "file icon themes" with **None** ("Disable File Icons"; the value `null`) and **Seti (Visual Studio Code)** (`vs-seti`, described by its id), from `theme.FileIconChoices()`. The item of the theme in use is active when the picker opens (None for `null` and for an id that is not known, since that shows no icons). **Preview:** moving the active item calls `Process.PreviewFileIconTheme(id)` ("" for None), which every window follows: `Window.FileIcons()` answers with the previewed theme while a preview is on, so the Explorer tree, Open Editors, the quick open, the recently opened and the editor switcher change at once (`Process.OnFileIconThemePreview` makes the windows refresh). None draws no icon column: a row is its label alone, with no room kept for the icon, and a file lines up with the name of the folders beside it. `Process.EndFileIconThemePreview()` goes back to the setting; a change of `workbench.iconTheme` in the settings of any window ends the preview and wins. **Enter** writes `workbench.iconTheme` (`null` for None, `"vs-seti"`) to the same file as the color theme (§8.4: the workspace's when its settings.json has the setting, else the user's); Escape and the other ways of closing the picker go back (§10.6 `OnHide`). The setting's type was already `string` or `null`. Not built: icon themes of extensions, `Product Icon Theme`, the "Install Additional File Icon Themes..." row.

### 10.9 Zen mode, full screen, zoom

- Zen mode (Ctrl+K Z) hides the activity bar, side bar, panel, status bar, the menu bar and (as `zenMode.showTabs` says) the tabs, centers the layout, and goes full screen (`zenMode.*` settings); full screen (F11); centered layout (`workbench.editor.centeredLayout*`). Editor font zoom (`editor.mouseWheelZoom`, the font zoom commands) changes only the editor's font size.
- **As built (step 8.5; `gui/kit/zoom.go`, `gui/app/{zoom,zen,centered}.go`, `gui/workbench/layout.go`, `core/settings/zoom.go`).**
  - *Zoom factor.* `settings.ZoomFactor(level)` is 1.2 to the power of the level (Electron's rule, VS Code's `zoomLevelToZoomFactor`); the level is clamped to -8..8 (`settings.ClampZoomLevel`; the reference's `MIN_ZOOM_LEVEL` is -8 and `MAX_ZOOM_LEVEL` 8), and `settings.ZoomStep` is what Zoom In/Out do: the level plus or minus one, rounded to a whole number (VS Code rounds so that no fractional level is made), and nothing when the new level is out of the range.
  - *`kit.Zoom`.* A widget between the navigator and the app (`WidgetsApp.Builder`, so the pages and every overlay, the menus, hovers, dialogs, the quick input and the notifications, are inside it): a `LayoutBuilder` gives an `OverflowBox` (as large as the room, so that the parents' bounds still contain every pointer position) a child `Transform.scale(factor)` of the child laid out with the constraints divided by the factor; layout, painting and hit testing go through the same matrix. Below it `MediaQuery.size` is the size divided by the factor and `devicePixelRatio` is multiplied by it. Text is drawn from glyph outlines under the transform, so it is as sharp as at its own size (checked with the golden `zoom_*` images); borders of 1 px are 1.2 px wide on the screen, so they are anti-aliased at fractional levels. The structure does not depend on the factor, so the state of the widgets is kept when the factor changes. Limits: a position in the window's coordinates (pointer events, `Bounds.Rect`, `FocusNode.Rect`) is the position in the overlay's times the factor; the places that put a widget at such a position use `kit.GlobalToOverlay` (the context menu does, as does the label that follows a dragged tab); a mouse wheel scrolls the same number of logical pixels, which are the factor times as many on the screen. The platform window is not changed (its size, its minimum size).
  - *Per window.* Every window has a zoom level (`Window.ZoomLevel`), set at its start from `window.zoomLevel` (application scope, default 0, -8 to 8) or, when it is zoomed by itself, from the state of the window. With `window.zoomPerWindow` (default true) Zoom In, Zoom Out and Reset Zoom change the level of the active window only and write nothing; Reset goes back to `window.zoomLevel`. With it false they write `window.zoomLevel` in the user's settings (Reset writes 0), and the other windows follow the setting. A change of `window.zoomLevel` sets the level of every window (a window that was zoomed by itself goes back to the setting). The level is saved in the window state (`zoomLevel`, only when it is not the setting's and the zoom is per window) and comes back at the next start.
  - *Commands, keys, menu.* `workbench.action.zoomIn` (Ctrl+=, Ctrl+Shift+=, Ctrl+NumPad_Add), `zoomOut` (Ctrl+-, Ctrl+NumPad_Subtract), `zoomReset` (Ctrl+NumPad0; titles Zoom In, Zoom Out, Reset Zoom), `workbench.action.toggleFullScreen` (F11), `toggleZenMode` (Ctrl+K Z), `exitZenMode` (Escape Escape while `inZenMode`; no title; weight `EditorContrib - 1000`, so any other Escape rule goes first and the second Escape of the chord is the only thing that leaves zen mode), `toggleCenteredLayout` (no key). The View > Appearance menu has Full Screen, Zen Mode and Centered Layout (group `1_toggle_view`, checked by the keys `isFullscreen`, `inZenMode`, `isCenteredLayout`) and Zoom In, Zoom Out and Reset Zoom (group `5_zoom`). The window keeps the context keys `isFullscreen`, `inZenMode` and `isCenteredLayout`.
  - *Full screen.* `Window.SetFullScreen` asks the controller of the Glutter window (`SetFullscreen`, reached by an interface assertion: docs/GLUTTER-ISSUES.md); the platform may refuse. The state is what the platform reports: the shell follows the controller (`readGeometry`) and tells the window (`fullScreenChanged`), which sets `isFullscreen` and saves `geometry.fullscreen` as before (§10.10; the window restores it only with `window.restoreFullscreen`). A window without a controller (not on screen) fails with `FullScreenUnavailable`. In full screen the menu bar of `window.menuBarVisibility` `classic` is hidden until the Alt key shows it (the `toggle` behavior), as VS Code does; `visible` keeps it.
  - *Zen mode.* The state is part of the layout (`workbench.LayoutState.Zen`, saved with it): whether it is on, which of the side bar, panel, activity bar and status bar zen mode hid (those that were shown), which of them the user changed meanwhile, and whether zen mode turned full screen, the centered layout and do not disturb on. Entering hides the side bar and the panel always, the activity bar with `zenMode.hideActivityBar`, the status bar with `zenMode.hideStatusBar`, and the menu bar (the workbench hides it while zen mode is on); `zenMode.fullScreen` goes to full screen unless the window is in it; `zenMode.centerLayout` turns the centered layout on; `zenMode.silentNotifications` turns do not disturb on (`notify.Service.SetDoNotDisturb`) when it was off. The tabs (`zenMode.showTabs`: `multiple`, `single`, `none`; VS Code's `zenMode.hideTabs` was replaced by it) and the line numbers (`zenMode.hideLineNumbers`) are not written to the settings: `Window.ShowTabs` and `Window.zenHidesLineNumbers` derive them from the layout and the settings, and the editor groups and the editors read them (the `OptionsFromSettings` of every editor gets `LineNumbersOff`). Ending it shows the hidden parts again, except those the user changed during zen mode (a part the user showed stays shown, and one that was shown and hidden again stays hidden; Toggle Status Bar Visibility and Toggle Activity Bar Visibility change the layout only while zen mode is on, not the setting); a setting that now hides the activity bar or the status bar takes it out of what comes back; it turns off what it turned on (full screen, the centered layout, do not disturb) and leaves what was on already. Leaving full screen (F11 or the platform) while zen mode made the full screen ends zen mode. The `zenMode.*` settings are followed live while zen mode is on. `zenMode.restore` (default true): a window that closed in zen mode starts in it again (the layout comes back as it was, do not disturb is turned on again, and full screen is asked for once the window has a controller); with it false the window starts with the parts back. Every window has its own zen mode. Not built: the "Tab Bar" submenu and the commands `workbench.action.zenHideEditorTabs`, `zenShowMultipleEditorTabs` and `zenShowSingleEditorTab`.
  - *Centered layout.* `LayoutState.CenteredLayout` is what was asked for (saved with the layout); the margins show when it is, and, with `workbench.editor.centeredLayoutAutoResize` (default true), the grid has no more than one column of groups, or a group is maximized (VS Code's `centerMainEditorLayout`; nothing is lost when a second group opens: the margins come back with the last one closed). The margins are in the color of the editor, around the whole editor area, tabs included (`centeredArea`): each is 0.1909 of the width, never less than 60 px, and the editor keeps at least the least width of a group; with `workbench.editor.centeredLayoutFixedWidth` the editor is 900 px wide (or the width of the area) and the margins share the rest. Not built: dragging the margins (the sashes of VS Code's centered view; double click to reset), the width of the editor kept per window, the diff editor's rule.
  - *Not built.* `editor.action.fontZoomIn`/`Out`/`Reset` keep their own keys and do not change the window zoom; `window.zoomLevel` for webviews; `window.density`; the zoom does not change the minimum size of the window or its real size; no status bar item shows the zoom level.

### 10.10 State, backups and restore (`core/storage`)

- Paths (XDG): user configuration in `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/emetos/` (default profile) or `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/emetos/profiles/<name>/`; state in `$XDG_STATE_HOME/emetos/` (with `profiles/<name>/` for a profile's UI state: the state files of its windows; the global state and the backups are those of the default profile, shared by all, see §10.12 "Profiles as built"); cache in `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/emetos/`; the socket in `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`. Every path is computed from these variables, so tests and proofs can redirect them (§15).
- **Window identity**: a window with a folder is identified by the first 16 hex digits of the SHA-256 of the folder's absolute path; a window without a folder gets a random id stored in the global state.
- UI state (global and per window id): open editors and their groups, view state per editor (selections, scroll, folds), layout, the explorer's expanded folders, window size, maximized and full-screen state, zoom level, recently opened folders and files. Saved as JSON on change (debounced 1 s, atomic replace) and on exit.
- **UI state as built (step 7.5; `core/storage`, `gui/app/state.go`, `gui/app/recent.go`).**
  - *Files.* `StateStore` (`NewStateStore(paths, profile, opts)`) reads and writes JSON files in the state folder of the profile: `state.json` (the global state) and `windows/<window id>.json` (one per window) (step 8.6 added `GlobalState.LastProfiles` and `OpenWindow.Profile`, §10.12; a window uses the store of its profile, `Process.StateFor`), every one with a `version` (`StateVersion` = 1) and with the permissions 0600 (the folders 0700). A file is replaced atomically (`WriteFileAtomic`: a temporary file in the same folder, fsync, rename). A file that cannot be read, is not JSON of the right shape, or has another version is ignored: the read reports the tag `StateFileUnreadable`, `StateFileCorrupt` or `StateVersionUnsupported` (the window logs a warning and starts as new), and the next save writes over it. Nothing is moved to a quarantine file.
  - *Window id.* `WindowID(folder)` is the first 16 hexadecimal digits of the SHA-256 of the cleaned absolute path; the window without a folder has the random id `StateStore.EmptyWindowID` makes once and keeps in `state.json`. Every window of a folder has the same file, so the state of a folder is not removed when the folder is closed, and is not removed ever (a folder that is gone leaves a file of a few kilobytes).
  - *Global state* (`GlobalState`): `emptyWindowId`; `lastWindow` (the folder of the window that opened last, or none for the window without a folder: an empty command line opens it again, `Process.startupFolder`); `geometry` (the size the last window had, for a window that has none of its own); `recent` (`RecentlyOpened`: the recent folders and the recent files).
  - *Recent lists* (`RecentlyOpened`, ported from `workspacesHistoryMainService.ts`): the most recent first, a path once (it moves to the front), at most 500 folders and 500 files (`MaxRecentEntries`), only absolute paths, and the files git makes for commit messages, merges and rebases (`COMMIT_EDITMSG`, `MERGE_MSG`, `git-rebase-todo`) are left out. A folder is added when a window opens it (at start and with `Window.SetFolder`), a file when an editor shows it (`Window.AddRecentFile`: the same moments the quick open list is added to). The lists keep the paths that are gone, but File > Open Recent and the picker only list the ones that exist (checked when they are shown: the menu stops at the first ten folders and ten files that exist, the picker looks in the background).
  - *Window state* (`WindowState`): `folder`; `geometry` (width and height in logical pixels, and `maximized` and `fullscreen`; the size is the one the window has when it is neither, which is the one it comes back to; no position is kept: Wayland compositors place windows); `layout` (`workbench.LayoutState` as JSON: the visibility, the sizes and the positions of the parts, the maximized panel, and since step 8.5 the centered layout and zen mode: `centeredLayout` and `zen`, which a state without them lacks); `zoomLevel` (the zoom level of the window when it is not the one of `window.zoomLevel`, §10.9; a number, absent for none, clamped when used); `grid` (`editorgroup.SerializedGrid`: the tree of groups with their sizes, the editors of each group with their pinned, preview and sticky marks, the order of use, the active editor of each group, the active group, the maximized group, and per editor its view state: `editor.ViewState` as JSON with its own `version`, with the selections, the scroll position and the folds); `explorer.expanded` (the expanded folders, paths relative to the folder, with slashes, sorted); `recent` (the files of the quick open list, §10.6). A saved path that is not absolute or leaves the folder, and a geometry that is not a size, are dropped when the file is read.
  - *Saving.* A change of the grid, the layout, the quick open list, the expanded folders, the size or state of the window, or the cursors or scroll position of an editor marks the state as changed. 250 ms later (a timer of the UI thread) the whole state is encoded on the UI thread and given to the store, which writes it after its own delay of one second (`DefaultStateDelay`; the timer is injectable) in a goroutine of its own; a burst of changes is one encoding and one write, and a file is never more than the delay behind however long the changes go on. A write that fails is logged and tried again by the next change and by the flush. The state is encoded and every file that waits is written at once, before the window closes (`Window.quit`, after the question about unsaved changes has been answered, and always when the shell is disposed) and at the end of `Run`. While the editors close because another folder is opened or the folder is closed (`openFolder`), saving is suspended: the state of the folder that is left stays as it was, and goes on under the id of the new folder. Opening a recent folder or choosing Open Folder does not restore the state of the folder that opens: restoring happens when a window starts (step 7.8 builds the rest).
  - *Restore* (`Window.PrepareRestore`, called by `Run` before the window is shown, and a no-op with `window.restoreWindows` = `none`, or when the window has no saved state). It reads the window state; applies the layout (`Layout.Restore` over the layout the settings make, so keys the file lacks keep their value) and the quick open list; keeps the expanded folders that still exist for the tree of the Explorer (`TreeModel.SetExpandedKeys`: the tree reads them as it comes on screen, which is how the folders of a restored tree load again); and opens, in the calling goroutine (the window is not on screen yet), the document of every saved text editor, once for a file in several groups. A saved editor whose file cannot be opened is dropped (the group keeps its other editors, their order of use and the active one; the preview and sticky marks follow), and one notification says how many: "1 editor could not be restored because its file no longer exists." and "N editors could not be restored because their files no longer exist.". The shell then makes the grid with `RestoreGridWith` over those documents, makes the editors, and gives each one its view state unless `workbench.editor.restoreViewState` is false (the cursors, the scroll position and the folds then start as for a new editor); a grid that cannot be read (`GridStateInvalid`) is logged and the window starts with one empty group. The files of the command line open on top of the restored editors, in the active group, and the first of them is shown; a file that is open already is only shown. The size and state of the window are the saved ones (`startupWindowConfig`: the first window is made with `WindowConfig{Size, State}`; a maximized window is restored always, a full-screen one only with `window.restoreFullscreen`), else the global geometry, else 1280x800. The window follows its size and state through the controller of Glutter's `WindowScope` (its listeners are told of every change of the metrics).
  - *Which window opens* (`Process.startupPlan`; as built in step 7.8 for several windows). The state keeps the windows that were open when the application last quit: `state.json` has `openWindows`, a list of `{id, folder}` in the order the windows were made, and `lastWindow`, the folder of the one that was active last. A window enters the list when it is made (and when its folder changes) and leaves it when the user closes it while others stay; a quit leaves the list as it is, so the next start opens the windows that were open at the quit. `window.restoreWindows` (application scope, default `all`) says which of them come back when the command line gives no folder and no file: `preserve` and `all` every window, `folders` the windows that had a folder, `one` the window that was active last, `none` an empty window (no state is read for it, though the state is still written, so that turning the setting back restores it); a window whose folder is gone is left out, and with no list (a state written before step 7.8) the last window is the one. With a folder or a file on the command line only the command line opens (the folder's own window, with its state, or a window without a folder, with its own state, and the files on top), except with `preserve`, which opens every restored window as well, the folder as one more window unless it was open (then that window gets the files), and the files in the window that was active last. VS Code's rule that a file on the command line replaces the restored editors of the window is not followed: the files always open on top. Whatever the setting says, a window of the list that has backups of unsaved documents opens too (hot exit, below), and with `none` and such windows no extra empty window opens. The first window of the plan is shown in the first Glutter window; the others are made with the first frame, and the window that was active last is the active one again once all are open.
  - *Window ids of several windows.* A window with a folder has the id of the folder, so a folder is never open in two windows: asking to open it comes to the front of the window that has it (a path through a link included). A window without a folder has a random id. The first one a first run makes keeps the id in `emptyWindowId` (as in step 7.5); the windows without a folder that New Window and Close Folder make have a fresh random id of their own, which is only in the list of the open windows, so a new empty window starts empty (no editors of another window come into it), and the state files of such a window that the user closes while others stay (state and backups) are deleted (`StateStore.DeleteWindow`); the ones left at a quit stay, and a start with `restoreWindows` that does not open them leaves their files where they are (a few kilobytes; not cleaned up).
  - *Menu and picker.* File > Open Recent (`MenubarRecentMenu`, group `2_open`, order 4) lists Reopen Closed Editor (dimmed until an editor was closed), the recent folders and the recent files as rows "~/projects/x" (the home folder as `~`, a label over 120 characters cut in the middle), More..., and Clear Recently Opened... A row runs `openRecentFolder` or `openRecentFile` with its path (commands without a title; a folder opens like Open Folder: the editors close first, with the question about unsaved changes of §10.3). `workbench.action.openRecent` (Open Recent..., Ctrl+R, also the row More...) shows the picker: "folders" and "files" as headings, the name as the label and the parent folder as the description (the filter looks at both), the first folder that is not the open one as the active row, and a button "Remove from Recently Opened" on every row. `workbench.action.clearRecentFiles` (Clear Recently Opened...) asks "Do you want to clear all recently opened files and workspaces?" ("This action is irreversible!", Clear / Cancel) and then clears both lists, the quick open list and the history of editors. Not built: the keys that move in the picker as a quick navigation and `workbench.action.quickOpenRecent`, the buttons that mark a folder open in a window or with unsaved files, opening in a new window with a modifier (step 7.8), workspaces.
  - *Not built.* The stacks of the navigation history and the recently closed editors, the active tab of the panel and the side bar's view container (only the layout), the state of the other kinds of editors (there are none yet).
- **Hot exit** (`files.hotExit`; `off`, `onExit` the default, `onExitAndWindowClose`; application scope):
  - Backups: one second after a dirty document's last change (each change restarts the delay), its content, encoding, byte order mark, line ending, language and the `FileStat` of the file it came from are written to `$XDG_STATE_HOME/emetos/backups/<window id>/<SHA-256 of the URI in hexadecimal>.json` (atomically, 0600; the same folder for every profile: backups belong to windows); a backup is removed when its document is clean again (saved, reverted, undone to the saved text), when it is closed with Save or Don't Save, or when `files.hotExit` is turned off.
  - `onExit` (default): when the application quits (Quit, or the last window closes) dirty documents are not asked about: they are backed up and restored on the next start. Closing a window that isn't the last asks about its dirty documents.
  - `onExitAndWindowClose`: as `onExit`, and closing a window that has a folder doesn't ask either (VS Code's rule; an empty window closing while others stay asks). Its backups are restored when its folder opens again.
  - `off`: always ask; no backups are made, and whatever closes takes its backups with it.
  - A crash: each window writes a `running` marker in its backup folder when it starts and removes it when it closes as it should; a marker found at start means the last run did not end, and that window's backups are restored whatever `files.hotExit` says (one notification says how many documents came back).
  - A backup whose file changed on disk since opens dirty with a notice.
- **Hot exit as built (step 7.6; `core/storage/backup.go`, `core/storage/backuptracker.go`, `core/document/restore.go`, `gui/app/backup.go`, `gui/app/backuprestore.go`, `gui/app/closing.go`).**
  - *Files.* `BackupStore` (`NewBackupStore(paths, window id, opts)`) holds the backups of one window in `backups/<window id>/`. A backup file is JSON: `version` (`BackupVersion` = 1), `uri` (`file:///path` or `untitled:3`), `content` (with `\n` line endings), `encoding`, `bom`, `lineEnding`, `language`, `stat` (`mtime` in nanoseconds, `size` and `mode` of the file the document last read or wrote; none for an untitled document) and `orphaned` (the file was gone). The name is the SHA-256 of the URI, so the backup of a URI is replaced and found without a list; a file whose name is not the hash of the URI it holds is corrupt. VS Code keeps the URI and the metadata on a first line of its own; Emetos keeps one JSON document. Only files and untitled documents that accept edits are backed up; a text over `MaxBackupSize` (100 MiB) is not backed up (`BackupTooLarge`) and the user is told once ("'x' is too large to be backed up. Its unsaved changes are lost if Emetos closes unexpectedly."). A write that fails is logged, told once ("The unsaved changes of 'x' cannot be backed up: ...") and tried again at the next change. Tags: `BackupWriteFailed`, `BackupEncodeFailed`, `BackupTooLarge`, `BackupURIInvalid`, `BackupReadFailed`, `BackupNotFound`, `BackupCorrupt`, `BackupDiscardFailed`, `BackupMarkerFailed` and `BackupWaitCancelled`.
  - *Consistency check* (`Check`, when a window starts, VS Code's `init`): the files of the folder that cannot be used (not JSON, another version, a name that is not the hash of the URI) and the temporary files of writes that did not finish are removed and logged; `List` skips them too and reports them. The listing is ordered by URI.
  - *Tracker* (`BackupTracker`, ported from `workingCopyBackupTracker.ts`): follows every document of the window (`Documents.SetObservers` tells it of each one that opens or closes). A document with unsaved changes gets a timer of `DefaultBackupDelay` (a change restarts it; the timers are injectable, tests move a fake clock); when it fires, the text is read on the UI thread and handed to one writer goroutine, which writes in order and replaces a write that waits by a newer one of the same document. A document that is clean again loses its backup (an empty untitled document has none); a document that closes loses it (Don't Save, or after Save); a document that gets another URI (a rename, a Save As) loses the backup of the old name and gets one under the new. An orphaned document is backed up with `orphaned` set. `SetEnabled(false)` (files.hotExit turned to `off`, live) cancels the timers and removes the backups; turning it on schedules the documents with unsaved changes. `Pause` and `Resume` bracket the time the window goes away: its documents close with it and keep their backups.
  - *Closing* (`closeDecision(hotExit, reason, hasFolder, otherWindows)` in `closing.go`, which replaces 7.2's `shouldAskBeforeClose`; `otherWindows` is whether other windows are open, as built in step 7.8):

    | `files.hotExit` | Quit, or the last window | another window closes, others stay |
    |---|---|---|
    | `off` | ask | ask |
    | `onExit` | back up | ask |
    | `onExitAndWindowClose` | back up | back up if the window has a folder, else ask |
    | any other value | ask | ask |

    "Back up" reads the texts of all the documents with unsaved changes on the UI thread, writes them on a goroutine (`BackupBatch`, bounded by 30 s), and closes without a dialog when every write succeeded; the backups are kept, the running marker is removed, and the editors that close with the window discard nothing. When a write fails (or a text is over the limit) the dialog of §10.3 asks instead (Save / Don't Save / Cancel); Cancel keeps the window open. When the dialog asks (the table, or the fallback), Save and Don't Save remove every backup of the window and the window closes; a window closed with nothing to lose removes them too when `files.hotExit` is `off`. Closing an editor, a group or a folder is not closing the window: it asks as in 7.2 and removes the backup of a document it closes (Save or Don't Save). The signal that ends the program (the context of `Run` is cancelled) writes the backups, if hot exit is on, and removes the marker.
  - *Marker and crash.* `PrepareRestore` writes `backups/<window id>/running` (the process id) and notes whether it was there. `Window.quit` removes it, so only a run that did not end leaves it. A leftover marker with backups gives one information notification ("Emetos did not close properly the last time. The unsaved changes of N files were restored from backups."). Backups are restored whether or not there is a marker and whatever `files.hotExit` is at start: a mode `off` at start restores what a run with another mode left (and then asks as `off` does, and removes the backups on exit). Every restored backup stays on disk until its document is saved, reverted or closed.
  - *Restore* (inside `Window.PrepareRestore`, before the window is shown; it runs even with `window.restoreWindows` `none`, which only leaves the layout and the editors out). The backups of the window id are read; the saved editors are opened as before, but a file that has a backup is read from the disk and the backup text is put over it as one edit (`Document.RestoreUnsaved`: dirty, one undo returns to the text of the file), with the encoding, byte order mark, line ending and language of the backup. A saved untitled editor (`untitled:3`) is recreated with that number (a new untitled document takes the lowest number that is free, never one that came back) and the backup text, language and encoding; without a backup it is dropped, without a notification. The groups, the order, the pinned and preview marks and the cursors, scroll positions and folds are the saved ones. A backup that no saved editor shows (the state file is missing or does not list it) opens as a kept editor at the end of the active group, in the order of the URIs, and the active editor stays. A backup whose text is the text of the file is removed; one that cannot be restored is logged and removed.
  - *File changed.* When the file on disk differs (size or modification time) from the stat in the backup, the document opens dirty with the backup text, with the stat of the backup (so the first save fails with `FileModifiedSince` instead of overwriting) and marked conflicted (§10.5): the sticky notification of the conflict is the only one, with the text "'x' has changed on disk since its backup was made. Your unsaved changes were restored; saving will ask before overwriting." and the buttons Overwrite and Revert.
  - *File gone.* A backup whose file is gone opens as a dirty document with the text of the backup, orphaned (struck through in its tab; a save makes the file again), with one warning notification for all such files: "'x' no longer exists on disk. Its unsaved changes were restored from a backup; saving will create the file again." ("N files no longer exist on disk. Their unsaved changes were restored from backups; saving will create the files again."). A file that exists but cannot be read now (it is binary, too large) also opens from the backup alone, not orphaned.
  - *Deviations from VS Code.* One JSON document per backup in place of the URI line and the content; the folder is `backups/<window id>/` with no `file` and `untitled` sub-folders and no `backups.json` model (the folder is the model); no backup of a read-only document; backups are restored without asking whether to; an empty window closing while others stay asks even with `onExitAndWindowClose` (VS Code's rule, which the first words of §10.10 do not state); the consistency check is by the name and the content of the files.
  - *Not built.* The backups of remote files, of other kinds of editors (there are none) and `window.confirmBeforeClose`.
- **As built in step 7.2 (closing).** `files.hotExit` is registered (`off`, `onExit`, `onExitAndWindowClose`; default `onExit`; scope application). `window.confirmBeforeClose` is not registered (the SPEC does not list it). Folder changes (Open Folder, Close Folder) always ask about unsaved changes through the dialog of §10.3. Step 7.6 made the decision "ask or back up" real (`closeDecision`, above).
- **Window close as built (step 7.2).** `Window.RequestClose(reason)` is the per-window flow, so step 7.8 can call it for every window: it runs `PlanClose` over all the editors of the window; with nothing to lose, or once the documents that auto save saves have been saved (§6.3 "Auto save": every mode but `off`, `SaveAuto`, no participants), or when `closeDecision` says to back up and the backups were written (step 7.6), the window closes at once; else the dialog of §10.3 asks, Save (all saved) and Don't Save close, Cancel and a failed save keep the window. Closing the last window ends the application: `Window.Quit` when it is set (tests count its calls), else `ExitApplication(AppExitTypeRequired)` on the binding; a window that is not the last is destroyed (step 7.8, below). A request while the dialog is open is ignored. The platform's request to close a window is answered "cancel" at once and the window then closes itself after the answer. **Glutter hook (step 7.8):** a regular window's close request goes to the `OnWindowCloseRequested` of its controller's delegate; the implicit first window's does not (`WindowingOwner.handleCloseRequested` skips it): it goes to `PlatformDispatcher.OnExitRequested` with the view ID and the binding turns it into a request to exit the app whose observers get no view ID. The manager takes that hook over (`Windows.bind`: `// Glutter:`), finds the window of the view and closes that window; a request to exit that has no window (`ExitApplication` with `AppExitTypeCancelable`, from the observers) is a quit. Recorded in `docs/GLUTTER-ISSUES.md`. `workbench.action.closeWindow` (Close Window; Alt+F4 and Ctrl+Shift+W; File menu row "Close Window", group `6_close`) and `workbench.action.quit` (Exit; Ctrl+Q; File menu row "Exit", group `z_Exit`, no palette entry) run the same flow with the reasons `CloseReasonWindow` and `CloseReasonQuit`; the process has one window, so Quit is the same as closing it. **Not built:** `window.confirmBeforeClose`.
- **Several windows as built (step 7.8): closing, quitting and restoring.** *One window* (`CloseReasonWindow`: its close button, Close Window, Alt+F4, Ctrl+Shift+W) asks only about its own documents (the dialog of §10.3, the questions of 7.2 and the hot exit table above with `otherWindows` true) and, once answered, is destroyed: its Glutter controller is unregistered and destroyed, its services are released (the backup tracker, the settings listeners), it leaves the list of the open windows and the next window becomes the active one; an extra window without a folder also loses its state. *The implicit first window* cannot be dropped from Glutter: when it closes while others stay its page is replaced by nothing (its shell and services are disposed) and its Glutter window is hidden (`SetHidden`; no surface, no entry in the task bar) until the application ends. *The last window* closing is a quit: the state is written and the application ends once (`Window.Quit`, else the manager's `Quit`, else `ExitApplication`). *Quit* (`CloseReasonQuit`: Ctrl+Q, Exit, a request to exit the application) asks every window in turn, in the order the windows were made, each in its own dialog, which comes to the front of its window; nothing is closed, backed up for good, discarded or written until every window has agreed. With hot exit on (`onExit`) all windows back up without a dialog. Cancel in any window stops the quit and keeps all windows and all their changes (what an earlier window answered "Don't Save" is not dropped: the answer counts only if the quit goes through); a window that cannot write its backups asks as 7.6 says. A request to close a window while the questions of a quit go on, a second quit, and a request while a dialog about closing is open are ignored. The signal that ends the program (the context of `Run` is cancelled) writes the backups and the state of every window, without questions. *Restore*: as above; each restored window runs `PrepareRestore` with its own id (state, then backups), so two windows of the same process restore independently, and a crash restores the backups of every window of the list.

### 10.11 Command line and windows (`cmd/emetos`, `core/ipc`)

- `emetos [options] [paths...]`: `-n`/`--new-window`, `-r`/`--reuse-window`, `-g`/`--goto file:line[:col]`, `-d`/`--diff <a> <b>`, `-m`/`--merge <path1> <path2> <base> <result>`, `-w`/`--wait` (exit when the files are closed, for `git commit`; with `--diff`, when the diff editor is closed), `--profile <name>`, `--log <level>`, `--version`, `--help`. A folder path opens it; file paths open the files. The options are parsed by hand (short and long forms, and positional counts for `--diff` and `--merge`).
- A running instance listens on `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/emetos-<uid>.sock` (versioned JSON messages); a second `emetos` hands its arguments and working directory over and exits, or waits with `--wait`. A stale socket (nobody listening) is removed. Two launches at once: a lock file makes exactly one of them the instance and the other hands over (as built, below).
- **Command line as built (step 7.7; `cmd/emetos/args.go`, `main.go`).** The parser is hand-written (`parseArgs`, no `flag` package). *Forms*: long options as `--name`, `--name value` and `--name=value`; short options as `-n`, `-n value` and `-nvalue`, with the boolean ones clustered (`-nw`, `-wg file:3`); `-h` and `-v` are the short forms of `--help` and `--version` (VS Code's; the old single-dash `-version` is gone). A value is the next argument whatever it looks like (`-g --odd`); `--` ends the options and everything after it is a path; a lone `-` (read standard input) is an error, `ArgsStdinUnsupported`. *Counts*: `--diff` takes two paths, `--merge` four (the first may be attached with `=` or to the short option, the others are the following arguments), `--goto`, `--profile` and `--log` one. `--goto` may be repeated (each adds a file); the other value options and `--diff`/`--merge` given twice are `ArgsRepeated`; the booleans may be repeated. *Positional paths* are literal: `main.go:3` is a file of that name (use `--goto` for a position); the first path that is a folder is the workspace to open (later folders are ignored) and the rest are files; a path that does not exist is a file, so opening it reports the error in the window. `--goto` is `file`, `file:line` or `file:line:column` (`^(.+?):(\d+)(?::(\d+))?$`; line and column count from 1, 0 is `ArgsGotoInvalid`; a value with no numeric suffix is a plain file). Every path is made absolute against the working directory of the launch before it is handed over. `--profile` takes 1 to 64 letters, digits, spaces, `-` and `_` (§10.12; `ArgsProfileInvalid`). *Errors* name the option (the tags are `ArgsUnknownOption`, `ArgsMissingValue`, `ArgsValueUnexpected` for `--wait=x`, `ArgsRepeated`, `ArgsConflict` for `-n` with `-r` and `-d` with `-m`, `ArgsStdinUnsupported`, `ArgsGotoInvalid`, `ArgsProfileInvalid`, `LogLevelInvalid`, `PathInvalid`); they are written to stderr with `Try 'emetos --help'.`. *Exit status*: 0 success, 1 failure (including an error reply of the running instance, which is printed to stderr), 2 a wrong command line; `--wait` ends with the status of the reply (0 when the files are closed). `--help` and `--version` print and exit 0 before anything else: no window, no socket, no log. The parser is in `cmd/emetos` and the typed request it makes (`ipc.OpenArgs`) is in `core/ipc`, which both `cmd/emetos` and `gui/app` import (no package was added to §5.2).
- **Single instance as built (step 7.7; `core/ipc`).** *Path*: `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/emetos-<uid>.sock` with `emetos-<uid>.lock` next to it (`ipc.Locations`, from `storage.Paths.SocketPath`); with no `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` they are `<temporary folder>/emetos-<uid>/emetos.sock` and `emetos.lock`, a folder made with mode 0700 that must be a real folder of the user and private (else `IPCRuntimeDirInvalid`); a path over 107 bytes (the address of a Unix socket) is `IPCSocketPathTooLong`. When the socket cannot be used, the editor starts without single-instance and says why on stderr. The socket has mode 0600 and the server serves only a peer of the same user (`SO_PEERCRED`, `IPCPeerRejected`). *Protocol*: one JSON object per line (newline-delimited), at most 1 MiB (`IPCMessageTooLarge`); the client sends `{"version":1,"type":"open","pid":N,"cwd":"/dir","open":{"folder","files":[{"path","line","column"}],"diff","merge","wait","newWindow","reuseWindow","profile"}}` and keeps the connection open and its write side open until the reply; the instance replies `{"version":1,"type":"ok"|"done"|"error","exitCode":N,"message":"..."}` (`ok` for a launch that does not wait, `done` for one that waited, `error` with exit code 1 and the message when it failed) and closes. A request of another version (`IPCVersionUnsupported`), an unknown type or one without `open` (`IPCTypeUnknown`) or a line that is not JSON (`IPCMessageInvalid`) gets an `error` reply and the server goes on; a request must arrive within 5 seconds, a reply to a request that does not wait within 30. A handler that panics is `IPCHandlerPanicked` (an `error` reply). *Two launches at once*: `ipc.Join` takes an exclusive `flock` on the lock file, tries to connect, and if nobody answers (no file, or `ECONNREFUSED`: a stale socket of a process that died, whose file is removed) listens before it releases the lock; a launch that holds the lock after that finds the socket, and the kernel queues its connection until the instance accepts, so exactly one launch becomes the instance and the others hand over. A path that exists and is not a socket of the user is left alone (`IPCSocketBusy`). The server closes by removing the socket file only if it is still the file it made (device and inode), under the same lock, so a later instance's socket is never removed. *`--wait`*: the connection stays open; the instance replies when every file that launch opened is closed (the document is closed when its last editor is) and every diff editor it opened is (the tab of the diff, whatever else shows its files), or when the window closes (exit code 0), or exit code 1 when files were asked for and none could be opened; a client that is killed or interrupted closes the connection, which cancels the wait. The instance serves clients concurrently; their launches reach the UI thread through `Launcher` (`gui/app`), and one that arrives before the window is on screen waits in a queue.
- **A launch in the running window as built (step 7.7; `gui/app/launch.go`, `Window.HandleLaunch`).** The window comes to the front (`Activate` of its controller, where it has one). `--diff` opens the diff editor over the two files in the active group (step 9.6: the files are read in the background, the first is the left side; with `--wait` the launch ends when the diff editor's tab is closed, with exit code 1 when a file cannot be opened; §9.11); `--merge` shows a notification ("The merge editor is not available yet.") and ends with exit code 1 and that message, and opens nothing else (`TODO(step 9.7)`). `--profile` is carried out by the manager of the windows, which picks or makes the window that has the profile (§10.12); a window that has another profile than the one asked for (a folder that is open with another profile, or `--reuse-window`) keeps its own and shows an information notification ("The folder is already open with the profile '<its profile>'." / "The window uses the profile '<its profile>'."). `--new-window` and `--reuse-window` are handled by the manager of the windows, which picks the window the launch opens in (`Windows.HandleLaunch`, below); the window itself only opens what it is given. A folder argument that is not the folder of the window (compared through links; only a launch handled by the window on its own, since the manager gives a window its own folder) opens as Open Folder does: every editor closes with the questions about unsaved changes (Cancel keeps the folder; the files still open), then the files open in the active group, kept, the first of them shown, with the cursor at the line and column of a `--goto` (a position past the end is clamped). The command line of the first instance is handled by the same code: its folder is the folder the window starts with, its files are opened on top of the restored editors (§10.10) with their positions, and its `--diff` opens the diff editor and its `--merge` gets its notice (its `--profile` chooses the profile of the window it opens, §10.12). Not built: the merge editor (9.7), reading standard input (`-`), `--add` and `--list-extensions`-style VS Code options.
- Multiple windows are Glutter windows of one process (§5.1; step 7.8 built them). **Opening a folder** (`Windows.OpenFolder`, `placeFolder`): a window that has the folder (through links) comes to the front, whatever else is asked; else `--new-window` opens a new window and `--reuse-window` replaces the window; else `window.openFoldersInNewWindow` (application scope, default `default`): `on` opens a new window, `off` replaces the window, `default` opens a new window unless the folder is picked from within the application (Open Folder, Open Recent, the button of the empty Explorer: those replace the window; VS Code's rule) or the window that would be replaced is empty (no folder, no editor), which a launch from outside replaces. The Ctrl key of the Open Recent picker (VS Code's `ctrlCmd`) opens the folder in a new window. Close Folder always replaces the window with a window without a folder. **Replacing the folder of a window** (`Windows.replaceFolder`/`replaceWindow`): the editors close first with the questions of §10.3 (Cancel keeps the window as it is, and so does a folder that cannot be opened, with a notification); then a new `Window` is made for the folder, the saved state of the folder is restored into it (layout, editors, view states, expanded folders; its backups), the Glutter window stays, and the old window is let go: its shell, watcher, file index, backup tracker (its backups were taken with its editors, and its running marker is removed) and listeners are disposed with the next frame, and the state of the folder that was left is the one it had when its editors began to close. A window that is not hosted by the manager (a test of one window) keeps the in-place `Window.SetFolder` of step 6.14. **A launch** (`Windows.HandleLaunch`, from the socket; the command line of the first instance is `startupPlan`): with a folder, the placement above (source: a launch), then the files open in the window that got the folder; without a folder, `--new-window`, or `window.openFilesInNewWindow` `on` (`default` is `on` for a launch; the default `off` on Linux), open a new window without a folder for the files unless `--reuse-window`; otherwise the files open in the window whose folder holds the first file (the deepest one), else the active window; `--new-window` alone opens an empty window; the target window comes to the front (`Activate`). `--wait` is tracked in the window the files opened in, and ends when they are closed or that window is. **Making a window** (`Windows.Open`): a folder that is open comes to the front; else the window is made on the UI thread (settings read, folder opened, saved state restored; those reads are the ones the first window does before it is shown), a regular Glutter window is created and registered, and the window is the active one. The size is the saved size of the folder's state, else `window.newWindowDimensions` (application scope, default `default`): `default` is the default size (1280x800), `inherit` and `offset` the size of the active window (Wayland lets the compositor place windows, so there is no offset: they are the same), `maximized` and `fullscreen` ask for that state after the window is made; the setting is not used for the first window. **New Window** (`workbench.action.newWindow`, Ctrl+Shift+N, File menu row "New &&Window", group `1_new`, order 3; VS Code's) opens a window without a folder, with nothing in it and a state id of its own. **Switch Window...** (`workbench.action.switchWindow`, in the palette, no key on Linux, as in VS Code) is a picker of the windows (rows named by the title of the window, "Current Window" on the row of the window the picker is in, the next window active); choosing a row brings the window to the front. Not built: the buttons of the switcher that close a window, the quick switch, the windows of other profiles, multi-root workspaces, placing windows by position.

### 10.12 Profiles

A profile is a name with its own `settings.json`, `keybindings.json`, `snippets/` (in its configuration folder) and UI state (in its state folder), §10.10. The default profile is the plain `emetos/` folders. Names are 1–64 characters of letters, digits, spaces, `-` and `_`. A window uses one profile: the one last used with its folder, else the default; `--profile` chooses it. Switching a window's profile reloads its settings, keybindings, snippets and theme in place (editors stay open). Backups belong to windows, not profiles. Export/import is one JSON file: `{"name", "settings", "keybindings", "snippets": {file name: text}}`, with the files' texts as strings.

- **Profiles as built (step 8.6; `core/storage/profiles.go`, `gui/app/profile.go`; the commands, pickers, status bar item and export/import are step 8.6b, below).**
  - *Names.* `storage.ValidProfileName`/`ValidateName` is the single rule (tag `ProfileNameInvalid`; `--profile` uses it for `ArgsProfileInvalid`): 1 to 64 characters, each a letter or digit (Unicode letters and digits are allowed), a space, `-` or `_`, with no space at the start or the end. `Default` in any capitalization is the name of the default profile and cannot be given to another one (`ProfileNameReserved`); two names that differ only in capitalization clash (`ProfileExists`). A profile is found by its name without regard to capitalization (`Profiles.Lookup`, which answers with the name as stored); `""` and `Default` are the default profile, whose identifier in `Paths` is `storage.DefaultProfile` (the empty string) and whose display name is `storage.DefaultProfileName`.
  - *Folders.* A profile is the folder `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/emetos/profiles/<name>/` (the default profile: `emetos/`). It holds `settings.json`, `keybindings.json` and `snippets/`; nothing else belongs to a profile. The user's color themes (`emetos/themes/`) are shared by all the profiles (§8.4). Its UI state is in `$XDG_STATE_HOME/emetos/profiles/<name>/windows/<window id>.json` (one file for each window, as §10.10 says); the global state (`state.json`: the recently opened lists, the open windows, the profile of each folder) and the backups exist once, in the folders of the default profile, and are shared by all the profiles ("backups belong to windows, not profiles"). `Profiles.List` gives the default profile first and then the folders of `profiles/` with a valid name, sorted without regard to capitalization; a folder with another name (a leftover `.new-*`) is not a profile.
  - *Creating.* `Profiles.Create(ctx, name, source)`: `storage.CreateEmpty`, or `storage.CreateFrom(profile)`, which copies `settings.json`, `keybindings.json` and the files of `snippets/` (regular files, folders inside it; links and other kinds are left out) and nothing else (not the themes, the state or the backups). "From the current profile" is `CreateFrom` with the profile of the window. The folder is built under a temporary name beside the others and renamed into place, so a failure leaves no half-made profile (`ProfileCopyFailed`, `ProfileCreateFailed`; the source must exist, `ProfileNotFound`). `Profiles.Ensure(name)` gives the profile, made empty when it does not exist (what `--profile` does, as VS Code does).
  - *Renaming and deleting.* `Rename` moves the configuration folder and the state folder (the first goes back when the second cannot move; `ProfileRenameFailed`) and rewrites the profile of the folders and windows in the global state, which is written at once. `Delete` removes both folders (`ProfileDeleteFailed`) and gives the folders and windows that had the profile the default profile. The default profile can be neither (`ProfileDefaultImmutable`). Whether a window uses the profile is the caller's to check (`Windows.UsingProfile`); the core does not know the windows (the commands of step 8.6b move the windows first, below).
  - *The profile of a folder.* `GlobalState.LastProfiles` (`lastProfiles` in `state.json`, additive: a state from before has none) maps the cleaned absolute path of a folder to the profile last used with it; the default profile has no entry. `Profiles.SetLastProfile(folder, profile)` and `LastProfile(folder)` (which answers only for a profile that still exists). A window with a folder notes its profile when it opens the folder and when it switches. `OpenWindow.Profile` (`profile` in the entries of `openWindows`, additive) is the profile each open window had, so that a restored window comes back with it and reads its UI state from that profile's folder; a profile that is gone gives the default profile.
  - *Which profile a window opens with* (`Windows.windowProfile`, `WindowSpec.Profile`): the profile of `--profile` (made when it does not exist; when it cannot be made the window uses the default profile and says so), else the profile of the entry of a restored window, else the profile last used with its folder, else the default profile. This follows this section and differs from VS Code, where a folder with no profile gets the profile of the window that was active last. Opening another folder in a window (Open Folder, Open Recent) applies the same rule to the new window; Close Folder keeps the profile of the window.
  - *`--profile` and the running instance* (`Windows.HandleLaunch`): a folder that is not open opens in a new window with the profile when the active window does not have it (a window without a folder and without editors is replaced instead, `--reuse-window` replaces the active window); a folder that is open in a window with another profile comes to the front and the window says "The folder is already open with the profile '<its profile>'."; files and a launch with no folder open in a new window with the profile when the window that would get them has another profile, else in that window (`--reuse-window` keeps the window and says "The window uses the profile '<its profile>'."). At the start the window the command line opens gets the profile; a window restored with another profile keeps it and says so.
  - *Switching* (`Window.SwitchProfile(ctx, name)`, on the UI thread; the profile must exist).
