The services that read the user's files belong to the window, so the switch moves them in place and nothing is closed: the user layer of the settings store (`Store.SetUserPath`: the store tells its listeners every setting whose value changed, so everything that follows the settings follows at once: the color theme of `Window.Themes`, the options of the editors, the tab sizes of new documents, auto save, the explorer, the layout settings, zoom, the window title, the settings editor, ...), the keybindings of the user (`userKeys` reads the new `keybindings.json` and replaces the resolver, so the new keys work, the old ones are gone and the keybindings editor shows the new rows; a pending chord is left), the snippet service (`Service.SetDir`), the files the watcher follows (the configuration folder of the old profile is not watched any more, that of the new one is), the documents that show a settings file (the `settings.json` and `keybindings.json` of the old profile that are open stay open as plain files; those of the new profile become the ones the settings and the keys are read from and edited through, §11.1), the state file of the window (the state is written to the old profile's folder first, then every later save goes to the folder of the new profile; nothing is read back, so the editors, groups, layout and view states stay), the profile of the folder, the list of the open windows and the observers of `Window.OnProfileChange`. The workspace layer of the settings (`.emetos/settings.json`) does not change. Two windows with two profiles have their own settings, keys, snippets and theme; a change of a user setting reaches the windows of the same profile only. A switch to the profile the window has is nothing; a file of the new profile that cannot be read is reported by the return value after the switch is complete.
  - *Limits.* The registry of the languages is the process's, so `files.associations` is that of the window that applied it last. `window.restoreWindows` is read from the default profile's `settings.json` before any window exists. Not built: profiles that hold the list of extensions (there are none), temporary profiles, settings sync.

- **Profiles UI as built (step 8.6b; `core/storage/profileexport.go`, `gui/app/profilecommands.go`, `statusbar.go`).**
  - *Commands* (all in the category Profiles, in the palette, with no default key, as VS Code binds none; registered by `registerProfileCommands`). VS Code ids: `workbench.profiles.actions.createProfile` "Create Profile..." (VS Code's title is "New Profile..." and it opens its profiles editor; here two quick inputs), `workbench.profiles.actions.switchProfile` "Switch Profile...", `workbench.profiles.actions.deleteProfile` "Delete Profile...", `workbench.profiles.actions.exportProfile` "Export Profile..."; and two that VS Code has only as buttons of its editor: `workbench.profiles.actions.renameProfile` "Rename Profile..." and `workbench.profiles.actions.importProfile` "Import Profile...". The command handlers are the window's (bound with the other commands of the shell). Not built: `newWindowWithProfile`, `createFromCurrentProfile` (Save Current Profile As), `manageProfiles` (Open Profiles (UI)), Show Profile Contents, Cleanup Profiles and Reset Workspaces; no context key and no setting is added; the palette hides none of the six.
  - *Create.* An input box ("Profile name") checked as the user types by `Profiles.CheckName` (the same rule as `Create`): the message under the box is "A profile name has 1 to 64 letters, digits, spaces, - or _, and no space at its start or end." (`ProfileNameInvalid`, which includes the empty text), "The name 'Default' belongs to the default profile." (`ProfileNameReserved`) or "A profile with the name 'X' exists already." (`ProfileExists`, without regard to capitalization); Enter on a name with a message does nothing, Escape cancels. Then a pick "Create profile from" with "Empty Profile" (active), and "Copy from <profile>" for every profile, the one of the window first and marked "Current", the others in the order of the list (Default first). `Profiles.Create` runs on a goroutine; then this window switches to the new profile with `Window.SwitchProfile` (VS Code switches too). A failure is an error notification "Cannot create the profile 'X': ...", a failed switch "Cannot switch to the profile 'X': ...".
  - *Switch.* A pick "Select Profile" of all the profiles, Default first, the current one active and with the description "Current"; Enter on the current profile does nothing, on another one switches the window in place (§ Switching above). VS Code sorts by name and marks the current with a check; Emetos keeps the order of `Profiles.List`.
  - *Rename.* A pick of the profiles that are not the default one ("Select Profile to Rename"; with none, an information notification "There are no profiles to rename."), then an input box with the old name in it and the same checks (its own name is allowed, so a change of capitalization is a rename; the same name accepted does nothing). Every window that uses the profile follows in place, nothing closes: the state of each window is written first (the state folder moves), `Profiles.Rename` renames the folders and the references of the global state, the stale state store of the old name is dropped (`Process.forgetState`), and each window runs `Window.profileRenamed(ctx, newName)`, which is the switch of `SwitchProfile` to the new name without writing the state first: the paths of the settings store, the keybindings, the snippet service, the watcher and the state file move to the new folders, the editors, documents and view states stay, the profile of the folder and the list of the open windows are updated and `OnProfileChange` is told (the status bar item and the title follow). A rename that fails is an error notification and nothing has moved. A document of the old profile's `settings.json` that is open keeps its old path (it is a plain file that is gone; as after a switch).
  - *Delete.* A pick of the profiles that are not the default one ("Select Profile to Delete"; with none an information notification "There are no profiles to delete."), one at a time (VS Code's `canPickMany` is not built: the quick input has no multiple choice), the current one marked "Current". A dialog asks "Are you sure you want to delete the profile 'X'? This cannot be undone." with the buttons Delete and Cancel (Cancel and Escape leave everything). On Delete every window that uses the profile switches to the default profile first, in place, then `Profiles.Delete` removes the folders and the references (a folder or an open window of the profile has the default profile), and the stale state store is dropped. A failure is an error notification "Cannot delete the profile 'X': ...".
  - *Export and import file.* One JSON file, `{"name", "settings", "keybindings", "snippets"}` in that order, written by `storage.WriteProfileExport` with a tab for the indentation, no HTML escapes and a line break at the end. `name` is the name of the profile ("Default" for the default one); `settings` and `keybindings` are the texts of the files of the profile (the empty string when the profile has none); `snippets` is an object of file name to text, `{}` when there are none. The snippets are the regular files at the top of the `snippets/` folder whose name is a plain file name ending in `.json` or `.code-snippets` (what the snippet service reads); folders inside it, links, other kinds of file and other extensions are not exported. The texts have to be valid UTF-8 (a file that is not is `ProfileExportUnsupported`) and the whole file has to be at most `MaxProfileExportSize` (16 MiB; `ProfileExportTooLarge`). The file is written atomically (a temporary file in the same folder, fsync, rename): a file that exists keeps its permissions, a new one has 0644. The texts survive byte by byte (a round trip gives identical files; line endings and a byte order mark included).
  - *Export.* A pick "Select Profile to Export" of all the profiles with the current one active, then the Save File dialog of the portal ("Export Profile", accept label "Export", suggested name `<name>.profile.json`, the filters "Emetos profile (*.json)" and "All files"), on a goroutine; reading the profile and writing the file are on that goroutine too. A dialog that is cancelled does nothing. The result is an information notification "Profile 'X' exported to <path>", or an error notification "Cannot export the profile 'X': ...". A desktop with no file chooser is `ProfileExportDialogFailed` (an error notification; there is no prompt for the path as Save As has).
  - *Import.* The Open File dialog ("Import Profile", accept label "Import", the same filters), then `storage.ReadProfileExport`/`ParseProfileExport`: at most 16 MiB (`ProfileImportTooLarge`) of JSON that is an object (`ProfileImportInvalid` for text that is not JSON, text after the value, another kind of value, a missing or invalid `name` (the rule of `ValidateName`), a `settings`, `keybindings` or snippet text that is not a string); a missing or `null` `settings`, `keybindings` or `snippets` is empty; unknown keys are ignored; **every snippet file name must pass `ValidSnippetFileName`** (`ProfileImportSnippetInvalid`): not empty, at most 255 bytes of valid UTF-8, no `/`, `\`, NUL or other control character, not `.` or `..`, a name before the extension, and the extension `.json` or `.code-snippets`, so a path (`../x.json`, `/abs.json`, `a/b.json`) can never leave the `snippets/` folder. A file that cannot be read is `ProfileImportReadFailed`; a desktop with no file chooser `ProfileImportDialogFailed`. A bad file is an error notification "Cannot import the profile: ..." and nothing is made. When the name in the file is free it is the name of the new profile; when it is taken (the name Default always is) an input box (the checks of Create) starts with a free one made by `Profiles.FreeName`: the name with " 2", " 3", ... added (VS Code adds no number; the profile names allow no parentheses, so not "(2)"). `Profiles.Import(ctx, export, name)` makes the profile like `Create` does, whole or not at all (the folder is built under a temporary name and renamed; `ProfileImportFailed`; the name errors of `Create`), with an empty `settings` or `keybindings` making no file and the files 0644; it runs on a goroutine, and the window then switches to the new profile (VS Code applies it too).
  - *Menus.* The submenu `ProfilesSubMenu` has the rows Create Profile..., Switch Profile..., Rename Profile... and Delete Profile... (group `1_manage`, orders 1 to 4) and Export Profile... and Import Profile... (group `2_share`, orders 1 and 2, after a line). It is a row "&&Profiles" in the Preferences submenu of the File menu and "Profiles" in the Manage menu, in the group `2_configuration` with order 1, so before Settings (VS Code's order and group; VS Code titles it "Profile (<current>)" and hides it while there are no profiles). Rename and Delete are always enabled; with no profile to act on they say so in a notification.
  - *Status bar.* The item `status.profile` (right side, priority 100: after the language item (100.1), before the bell; name "Profile") shows `$(account) <name>` with the tooltip "Profile: <name>" and runs `workbench.profiles.actions.switchProfile`. It is visible only while the profile of the window is not the default one, and `Window.OnProfileChange` keeps it up to date (a switch, a rename). VS Code shows the profile in the activity bar and the window title instead.
  - *Title.* `${profileName}` of `window.title` (§10.1) is the name of the profile of the window, empty for the default profile (VS Code's); the title is made again when the profile changes.
  - *Not built.* Selecting several profiles to delete, a profile with other contents than the three files (§10.12 first paragraph), a prompt for the path when the desktop has no file chooser, temporary profiles, sharing a profile by a link or a gist, the `.code-profile` format of VS Code (it includes extensions, tasks and global state).

### 10.13 Panels: Output and Problems (`gui/views/output`, `gui/views/problems`)

The panel (§10.1) has one tab per view container of the `panel` location of the view registry, in order: **Problems** (`workbench.panel.markers`, the `warning` Codicon, order 0, with the view `workbench.panel.markers.view`) and **Output** (`workbench.panel.output`, the `output` Codicon, order 1, with the view `workbench.panel.output`); the Debug Console and the Terminal come with their steps. A tab shows its title in capitals (11 px) with a line under the active one; the Problems tab also shows the count of problems (errors, warnings and infos, whatever the filter) in a pill in `panelTitleBadge.background` and `panelTitleBadge.foreground` (VS Code's `NumberBadge`; "Total N Problems" on hover, none when there are no problems, "999+" above 999). `workbench.Views` (step 6.7) also keeps the container the panel shows (`ActivePanel`, `OpenPanel(id)` shows the panel and the tab); a click on a tab opens it. `workbench.Options` has `PanelTabs` (with `Badge` and `BadgeTooltip`), `PanelActions` (the actions of the open view, in the title strip before the maximize and close buttons, as VS Code's view title actions), `FocusPanelView` (called with the container once a command has opened it with the focus) and the existing `Panel` body. The window keeps the two views for as long as it is shown (`Window.Problems`, `Window.Output`), so what a tab holds (the filter, the expansion of the tree, the channel, the scroll lock) survives a change of tab.

**Output** (`output.View`, `NewBody`, `NewActions`). The channels are those of `core/logs` (§13): `Store` keeps the last `DefaultMaxLines` (10,000) lines of each channel, `Watch(channel, fn)` gives the lines and then every event (`EventChannelAdded`, `EventLine`, `EventCleared`) with no gap and no repeat, and `Clear(channel)` empties a channel, which stays. `Process.Channels` is the store of the log (the Emetos channel is `logs.MainChannel`), or one of its own when the process has no log file. The view shows the active channel in a **read-only editor** (the real editor widget over a `document` with the virtual URI `emetos-virtual:output`; the document is made writable only while the view appends, its history is dropped after each edit, and it never counts as dirty), with VS Code's options for an output text: word wrap on, no line numbers, glyph margin, folding, minimap, sticky scroll, rulers or current-line highlight, 20 px of line decorations. Lines are queued by the logging goroutine and shown on the UI thread, many in one go (`binding.Post`); a channel older than the limit loses its first lines as the store does; a message with line breaks is several lines. **Follow tail**: after lines are added the editor scrolls to the end (after the next frame, since its layout lags the text) while the view is not locked and was showing the end before the lines came (a view the user scrolled up stays where it is), whatever the position when `output.smartScroll.enabled` is off. The **scroll lock** (`outputView.scrollLock`) keeps the view still; it is toggled by the lock button and `workbench.output.action.toggleAutoScroll`, locked by a click on a line other than the last and unlocked by a click on the last line (the smart scroll, a cursor move that is not the focus changing), and unlocking goes to the end. Switching channel unlocks and shows the end. The title strip has the dropdown of the channels (a channel that appears while the panel is open is added), the Clear button (`workbench.output.action.clearOutput`, which empties the channel in the store) and the lock button (the `lock` Codicon, "Turn Auto Scrolling Off"; `unlock`, "Turn Auto Scrolling On", while locked). `workbench.action.openActiveLogOutputFile` opens the log file in an editor for the Emetos channel (a notification says that another channel has no file). Not built: the filter box and the log level actions, Add Compound Log, Remove Output, Show Output Channels and Show Logs (they need the quick input), save, import and export of logs, "Open Output in New Window", the context menu of the Output editor (`inOutput` is set while it has the focus), links in the text.

**Markers** (`problems.Service`, `Marker`, `Severity`, in `gui/views/problems`; VS Code's `IMarkerService`). No package of §5.2 holds markers, so the model lives with the panel that shows it, in files that do not import Glutter; a producer of area 3 (language servers, tasks) sets markers on the window's `Window.Markers` from `gui/app`. A `Marker` has an owner, a resource (the absolute path of the file), a severity (`SeverityHint` 1, `SeverityInfo` 2, `SeverityWarning` 4, `SeverityError` 8, VS Code's values), a code, a source, a message, a range (`StartLine`, `StartColumn`, `EndLine`, `EndColumn`, counted from 1; a column counts UTF-16 units) and related information. `Set(owner, resource, markers)` replaces the markers of an owner for a file (none removes them; a marker with no message is dropped, and a range is made valid: positions from 1, the end not before the start); `Remove(owner, resources...)` removes those of the given files or of all of them; `Read(ReadFilter{Owner, Resource, Severities, Take})` gives markers sorted by seriousness (errors first), then resource and position; `Stats()` counts errors, warnings, infos and the rest; `Subscribe` is told which resources a change touched (on the goroutine that changed them; the service is safe for concurrent use). The hints are not shown in the panel.

**Problems** (`problems.Model`, `View`, `NewBody`, `NewActions`). The `Model` groups the markers by file, sorted as VS Code sorts them (a file whose first marker is more serious comes first, then by path; the markers of a file errors first, each severity by position), narrows them with a `FilterOptions` and counts the total and the filtered. It refreshes on the UI thread, once for any number of changes. **Filter**: the severity switches (errors, warnings, infos), the active file only (`SetActiveFile` is told by the window which file the active editor shows), and the text of the filter box, which as in VS Code's `FilterOptions` is words that match, case-insensitively, the message, source and code of a marker and the name of its file (a match on the name shows all the markers of the file); a leading `!` shows what does not match; the patterns separated by commas are globs (`**/*.go`, `.go` for `*.go`, `pkg` for a folder anywhere; they are matched against the whole path) that include files, and a `!` before one excludes its files; `@source:name` (quoted when it has spaces, `!@source:name` to exclude) keeps the markers of a source. Not built: fuzzy matching of the words (they match as substrings), the exclusion by `files.exclude` (`FilterOptions.Exclude` takes it; there is no setting before step 6.13), the table view and its commands (`problems.defaultViewMode`), the multi-line messages, the related information rows, the quick fix and copy actions, `problems.autoReveal`, `problems.sortOrder` and `problems.showCurrentInStatus` (no consumer yet), and the settings `problems.visibility` and `problems.decorations.enabled` (they hide the squiggles and the status of area 3). The tree (`kit.Tree` over the model, 22 px rows) has a row per file (a file icon placeholder (the file icon theme of step 6.14, §10.8, is not used here yet), the name, the folder dimmed and relative to the folder of the window, and a count `kit.Badge`) and a row per marker below it (the icon of the severity in `problemsErrorIcon.foreground`, `problemsWarningIcon.foreground` or `problemsInfoIcon.foreground`, the first line of the message, the source and code dimmed as `source(code)`, and the position `[Ln 3, Col 5]` dimmed); a file that gets markers is expanded, and one the user folded stays folded while it has markers. A click on a file folds or unfolds it; a click on a marker, or Enter, opens the file as a preview in the active group with the range of the marker selected (the file is read in the background; a column is converted from UTF-16 units to bytes; a double click keeps the editor). Without markers the body says "No problems have been detected in the workspace." (or "... in the current file." with the active file only), and when the filter lets nothing through "No results found with provided filter criteria." with a "Clear Filters" link. The title strip has the filter box (placeholder "Filter (e.g. text, **/*.ts, !**/node_modules/**, @source:ts)"), a switch for each severity and one for the active file, and the Collapse All button; the switches show VS Code's "more filters" menu as separate buttons.

**Commands** (ids, titles and keys of VS Code, Linux): `workbench.actions.view.problems` "Toggle Problems" (Ctrl+Shift+M) and `workbench.action.output.toggleOutput` "Toggle Output" (Ctrl+K Ctrl+H: VS Code binds Ctrl+Shift+U, and Ctrl+K Ctrl+H on Linux, where the desktop takes Ctrl+Shift+U) show the tab with the focus, or hide the panel when its view has the focus; `workbench.panel.markers.view.focus`, `workbench.panel.output.focus` and `workbench.action.problems.focus` show a tab with the focus; `workbench.actions.view.toggleProblems` hides the panel when the Problems are shown, else shows them with the focus; `workbench.action.showErrorsWarnings` shows them without the focus. All but the toggles are in the View menu: Problems and Output, in the group `5_panel` after Editor Layout. The commands of the Problems view: `problems.action.focusFilter` (Ctrl+F while `focusedView == 'workbench.panel.markers.view'`), `problems.action.focusProblemsFromFilter` (Ctrl+Down while `problemsFilterFocus`), `problems.action.clearFilterText` (Escape while `problemsFilterFocus`), `workbench.actions.treeView.workbench.panel.markers.view.collapseAll` and `workbench.actions.workbench.panel.markers.view.toggleErrors`, `.toggleWarnings`, `.toggleInfos` and `.toggleActiveFile`; and of the Output view: `workbench.output.action.clearOutput`, `workbench.output.action.toggleAutoScroll` (VS Code's id: this version of VS Code has no `toggleOutputScrollLock`) and `workbench.action.openActiveLogOutputFile`. **Context keys** (set on the window's keys): `activePanel` (the container the panel shows) and `problemsVisibility` (the panel is shown with the Problems) by the workbench; `focusedView` (the view that has the focus: `workbench.panel.markers.view` or `workbench.panel.output`), `problemFocus` (the tree), `problemsFilterFocus`, `problems.filter.errors`, `problems.filter.warnings`, `problems.filter.info` and `problems.filter.activeFile` by the Problems; `inOutput`, `outputView.scrollLock` and `activeOutputChannel` by the Output. **Colors**: `problemsErrorIcon.foreground`, `problemsWarningIcon.foreground` and `problemsInfoIcon.foreground` (step 6.5), and with this step `activityBarBadge.background`, `activityBarBadge.foreground`, `panelTitleBadge.background` and `panelTitleBadge.foreground`; the tree and the editor use the list, input, badge and editor colors. **Settings**: `output.smartScroll.enabled` (true).

### 10.14 One window: workspace tabs and the system tray (`gui/app`, `gui/workbench`)

Emetos has **one window** (D17). Every folder it opens is a **workspace tab** in a strip at the very top of that window, and the window can go to a **system tray** icon instead of closing. This replaces the several windows of step 7.8: what §10.10–§10.12 and the "as built" notes of steps 7.5–8.6 say of a *window* (its folder, profile, settings store, documents, editor groups, layout, quick input, notifications, backups, UI state, its forked command registry) is from milestone M9b on said of a *workspace tab*; what they say of the process (the registries, the themes, the languages, the logs, the profiles, the socket) stays the process's. Where a section of the SPEC still says "window" for those, read "workspace tab".

**The model.** `app.Workspace` (the type that was `app.Window`) holds one workspace: a folder or none, its profile and every per-window service of step 7.8. `app.Workspaces` (that was `app.Windows`) holds the tabs in their order, the active one, and opens, replaces, moves and closes them. The one Glutter window (the implicit window of `RunApp`; no `RegularWindowController` window is made any more) shows the title bar row and, under it, the workbench of the active tab. The tabs that are not active stay alive (their documents, watchers, file index, auto save, backups and, in later areas, their language servers run), and keep their widget trees mounted with their tickers off and nothing painted (an `IndexedStack` of the tabs' workbenches under a `TickerMode`), so that switching back is instant and keeps the scroll positions, the focus within the tab and the state of every view. Keyboard focus, context keys and the key dispatcher belong to the active tab; switching tabs moves the focus to what had it in that tab.

**The title bar row** (Emetos draws it: `RunAppOpts` `Decorations: DecorationsApp`, with `widgets.WindowDecoration` for the borders, the resize edges, the drag, the double click and the window menu; 35 px high; `titleBar.activeBackground`/`titleBar.inactiveBackground`, `titleBar.activeForeground`/`titleBar.inactiveForeground`, `titleBar.border`). From left to right:
- **The menu.** `window.menuBarVisibility` gains the value `compact` (VS Code's), the new default: a `≡` button that opens the menus (File, Edit, Selection, View, Go, Help) as one menu with submenus. `classic` and `visible` show the menu titles in the row before the tabs; `toggle` shows them after the Alt key; `hidden` shows none (the menus stay reachable with Alt and F10). There is no separate menu bar row any more (§10.1).
- **The workspace tabs.** Each tab shows the Codicon `root-folder` (a folder tab) or `window` (the No Folder tab), the name, a dot when any document of the workspace has unsaved changes (the dot is replaced by the close button while the pointer is over the tab), and a close button on the active tab and on the tab under the pointer. The name is the folder's base name; when two tabs have the same name, each adds the shortest distinguishing part of its parent path after an em dash (`api — work`, `api — home`, as VS Code's label disambiguation). The tooltip is the full path, and `Profile: <name>` on a second line when the profile is not the default. The active tab has `tab.activeBackground` and `tab.activeForeground` with a 1 px top border in `tab.activeBorderTop` (or `focusBorder`); the others `tab.inactiveForeground` on the row's background. A tab is at least 120 px and at most 240 px wide; when the tabs do not fit they shrink to 120 px and then the strip scrolls horizontally (wheel, and a drag at its ends), with the active tab kept in view. A middle click closes a tab; a click on `+` after the last tab opens a new No Folder tab; a double click on the empty part of the strip maximizes or restores the window; a drag of the empty part moves the window. The context menu of a tab: Close, Close Others, Close to the Right, Copy Path, Reveal in File Manager (the `OpenURI` portal on the folder), and Switch Profile... (§10.12).
- **The window buttons** that the compositor allows (minimize, maximize or restore, close), as `WindowDecoration` draws them.

The window's own title (what the task bar shows) is the title of the active tab's window title template, as before (`window.title`, §10.1). In full screen and zen mode the row hides with the menu bar as the menu bar did.

**Opening folders and files** (`Workspaces.Open`; it replaces the placement rules of step 7.8). A launch (the first instance's command line, a hand-over over the socket, §10.11) and the commands inside Emetos open folders as tabs:
- A folder that a tab has (compared through links) becomes the active tab, whatever else is asked.
- Otherwise `--reuse-window` (`-r`) opens the folder in the active tab (as Open Folder in a window did: the editors of the tab close with the questions about unsaved changes; Cancel keeps the tab as it was), and `--new-window` (`-n`) opens a new tab after the active one.
- Otherwise `window.openFoldersInNewWindow` decides, read as "in a new tab": `on` a new tab, `off` the active tab, `default` a new tab, except that an active tab with no folder and no editors (an untouched No Folder tab) is replaced. Open Folder and Open Recent follow the same rule (the Ctrl key of Open Recent forces a new tab); Close Folder turns the active tab into a No Folder tab.
- Files without a folder (`emetos notes.txt`, `--goto`) open in the active tab; `--diff` and `--merge` open their editor in the active tab.
- A launch shows the window if it is hidden and brings it to the front (`SetHidden(false)` and `Activate`).
- `--profile` (§10.12): a folder that is not open opens in a new tab with that profile; a folder open in a tab with another profile becomes the active tab and the tab says "The folder is already open with the profile '<its profile>'."; files and a launch without a folder use the active tab when it has the profile, else a new No Folder tab with it.
- `workbench.action.newWindow` (Ctrl+Shift+N, title "New Workspace Tab") opens a No Folder tab; `workbench.action.closeWindow` (Ctrl+Shift+W, title "Close Workspace Tab") closes the active tab; `workbench.action.openFolderInNewWindow`-style commands open the folder in a new tab.

**Closing a tab** asks, as closing a window did, about the documents of the tab with unsaved changes (or keeps them as backups by `files.hotExit`, §10.10), then disposes the workspace (its watchers, its services, its state file is written last). Closing the last tab leaves one No Folder tab showing the home page (Open Folder, Open Recent, the recent list); Emetos never has zero tabs.

**The tray** (`desktop.NewSystemTray` of Glutter, StatusNotifierItem with a dbusmenu menu). The icon is the application icon (§17; the icon program of step 9b.4 draws it), the tooltip "Emetos" with the number of workspaces under it. A click on the icon shows the window and brings it to the front when it is hidden or not in front, and hides it when it is in front. The menu: one item per workspace tab (its name; choosing it shows the window with that tab active), a separator, Open Folder..., New Workspace Tab, a separator, Show Emetos / Hide Emetos, Quit.
- **The window's close button hides the window** to the tray (`RegularWindowController.SetHidden(true)` or the implicit window's equivalent; the window has no surface and no frames are drawn while it is hidden) when `window.closeToTray` (application scope, boolean, default `true`, new) is on and a tray is shown; the workspaces keep running. Before hiding, the backups of hot exit are written at once (§10.10). When no desktop shows tray icons (`TrayUnavailable`, plain GNOME without the AppIndicator extension), or `window.closeToTray` is off, the close button quits.
- **Quit** (File > Exit, Ctrl+Q, the tray's Quit, the close button without a tray) is the quit of §10.10 over every tab: hot exit backs up and quits, or, with `files.hotExit` off, the questions about unsaved changes are asked tab by tab (the tab in question becomes active and the window is shown first).
- `emetos` with no argument, when an instance runs, shows the window.

**Restore** (§10.10). `state.json` keeps `workspaceTabs` (in order: the folder or none, the profile, a stable tab id) and `activeWorkspaceTab`; the UI state of each tab is the window state file of §10.10 (`windows/<window id>.json`, by the folder as before, in the profile's state folder). A `state.json` written by an older Emetos (its `openWindows` list) is read as the list of tabs. `window.restoreWindows` is read as "restore tabs": `all` and `preserve` bring back every tab, `folders` the tabs with a folder, `one` the active tab only, `none` one No Folder tab. The window's own size, position and maximized state are saved once (`windowGeometry` in `state.json`).

**Switching tabs.** `emetos.workspaceTab.next` (Ctrl+Alt+PageDown) and `emetos.workspaceTab.previous` (Ctrl+Alt+PageUp) cycle; `emetos.workspaceTab.open1` ... `open9` (Ctrl+Alt+1 ... Ctrl+Alt+9; Alt+1 ... Alt+9 stay VS Code's `workbench.action.openEditorAtIndex1..9`) go to the tab at that place (9 is the last); `emetos.workspaceTab.switch` "Workspace: Switch Workspace Tab..." opens a quick pick of the tabs (name, path, profile, a dirty mark; the active one first; typing filters with the fuzzy scorer, §10.6); `emetos.workspaceTab.moveLeft`/`moveRight` move the active tab; tabs are reordered by dragging (the drag of step 6.10's editor tabs: 4 px slop, Escape cancels). Context keys: `workspaceTabCount`, `activeWorkspaceTabHasFolder`.

**Not built:** several windows (D17); dragging a tab out into a window of its own; a tab for a multi-root workspace (Emetos has one folder per tab); pinning workspace tabs; colors of their own for workspace tabs (they reuse the editor tab and title bar colors); a folder's own icon on its tab; starting with the desktop session (autostart) and starting hidden.

## 11. Configuration

### 11.1 Settings (`core/jsonc`, `core/settings`)

- `settings.json` files are JSON with comments and trailing commas. `core/jsonc` parses them with positions, and edits a value while keeping comments and formatting (as VS Code's `jsonc-parser` does), given formatting options (tab size, spaces, line ending).
- Layers, lowest to highest: defaults, user (profile), workspace (`<folder>/.emetos/settings.json` only, D12), and language-specific blocks (`"[go]": {...}`) inside each layer.
- Every setting is registered with key, type, default, description, enum values and descriptions, range, scope, and category, by the step that first uses it, with VS Code's values (`editorOptions.ts` for `editor.*`, the workbench's configuration contributions for the rest). Reading an unregistered key is a programmer error (a panic in debug builds). Unknown keys in a file are kept and reported. Change events carry the affected keys. Writing a value creates the file and its folder when needed.
- **Resolution** (as VS Code): the layers are merged (objects key by key, anything else replaced) and then the `[language]` blocks of the merged result are applied for the language asked about, so a block of any layer wins over the plain settings of every layer, a workspace block over a user block, and a block of one language alone over a block shared with others (`"[go][json]"`). A language's defaults (a language contributes them like VS Code's `configurationDefaults`) are blocks of the defaults layer. Settings of the application scope are read from the user file only; a workspace file that sets one gets a problem and the value is ignored. A value that its setting does not allow (type, enum, range) falls back to the default and is listed as a problem; so are syntax errors, unknown keys and ignored keys. Writing a value keeps the comments and the file's own indentation, line ending and byte order mark, edits a setting written as nested objects where it is, and creates the file and its folder.
- The settings UI shows registered settings by category with search, edits user or workspace values, and links to the JSON (step 8.1, below).

**The settings editor as built (step 8.1; `core/settings/{layout,display,uimodel,textsource}.go`, `gui/settingsui`, `gui/app/{settingspane,settingsdocs}.go`).** Ports of `settingsEditor2.ts`, `settingsTree.ts`, `settingsTreeModels.ts`, `settingsLayout.ts` and `preferencesValidation.ts`.
- **Groups.** `DefaultTOC()` is VS Code's table of contents (`tocData`: Text Editor with Cursor, Find, Font, Formatting, Diff Editor, Multi-File Diff Editor, Minimap, Suggestions and Files; Workbench with Appearance, Breadcrumbs, Editor Management, Settings Editor, Zen Mode and Screencast Mode; Window with New Window; Features with Explorer, Search, Problems, Output and Merge Editor; Application; Security) without the groups that no setting of Emetos can be in (Chat, Debug, Terminal, Notebook and so on, which come back with the setting that needs one; the Diff Editor group is shown since step 9.2, which registers `diffEditor.maxComputationTime`). Each group has the key patterns of VS Code (`*` matches anything, whatever the case) and `BuildCategories` places the registered settings as VS Code's `resolveSettingsTree` does: the groups below a group take their settings first, then the group takes those of its patterns that no group has taken, sorted by key (case does not decide; there is no `order`). **Commonly Used** is the first group and has copies of its settings (VS Code's list, the ones that are registered). A setting no group claims goes in **Extensions**, in a group named after the first segment of its key (`emmet.*` is "Extensions > Emmet", where VS Code has the Emmet extension). A group with no setting and no group below it is not shown, and the Workspace tab leaves out the settings a workspace cannot set (the application and machine scopes), as VS Code does. The `Category` field of a setting is not used for the layout.
- **Titles.** `KeyToDisplayFormat(key, groupID)` is `settingKeyToDisplayFormat`: the part of the key before its last dot is the category, written as words (`WordifyKey`: dots become " › ", camel case is split, acronyms and a few product names are capitalized) after the part that the group already says is cut off, and the rest is the label: `editor.fontSize` is "Editor: Font Size" everywhere but in the Font group, where it is "Font Size"; `workbench.editor.showTabs` is "Workbench › Editor: Show Tabs" and "Show Tabs" in Editor Management. A description is the Markdown of the registry rendered as far as the descriptions use it (`RenderDescription`: code spans in the editor's font, `[label](target)` links and `#setting.key#` references drawn as links that do nothing yet, `**` dropped).
- **Rows** (`UIModel.Row`, `RowKind`). A check box for a boolean, a number box for a number or an integer, a text box for a string, a list (`kit.Dropdown`, whose rows show the descriptions of the values) for a setting with `Enum` of the types VS Code renders as a list, and for the rest (arrays, objects, a value of several types such as `workbench.iconTheme`) the link "Edit in settings.json". The value shown is the one in the file of the tab when it sets the setting, else the default (the Workspace tab shows the default of a setting only the user file sets, with "(Also modified in: User)" after the title; the User tab says "Workspace" likewise). A row whose setting is in the file of the tab is **modified**: a 2 px bar in `settings.modifiedItemIndicator` at its left, and the gear (visible while the pointer is over the row or it has the focus, as VS Code shows its toolbar) opens the menu Reset Setting (removes the key from the file with `Store.Remove`, which keeps the comments of the other lines; VS Code's removal also drops the comments that sit next to the key), Copy Setting ID and Copy Setting as JSON; a right click on the row opens the same menu. A value in the file that the setting does not allow is shown as the default with the message "The value in settings.json is not allowed: ... Fix in JSON.". A row is as high as its text and control are (the list is a column in a `kit.ScrollableElement`; its rows are about 180, so it is not virtualized).
- **Committing.** A check box and a list write at once. A number or text box writes on Enter and when it loses the focus, when its text differs from the value shown; a number is checked as the user types, with VS Code's messages ("Value must be a number.", "Value must be greater than or equal to 1.", "Value must be less than or equal to 100.", "Value must be an integer."), shown in a box in `inputValidation.error*` below the input, and a text that is not allowed is kept in the box and not written. The write is `Store.Update` into the user's or the workspace's file (the tab); a write that fails (the file has syntax errors: tag `SettingsFileInvalid`) is reported in a notification and the row keeps showing the old value. The rows follow the store: a change by anything else (typing in `settings.json` and saving, the Auto Save toggle, another window) shows at once, and the text of a box that the user is typing in is replaced only when the value of the setting changed.
- **The page.** A search box ("Search settings", with the filters of step 8.2 below; the Commonly Used group is left out while a search shows), the User and Workspace tabs (the Workspace tab is dimmed, with a tooltip, without a folder, and a saved view of it then shows the User tab), the button that opens the file of the tab (the `go-to-file` icon, "Open Settings (JSON)"; `workbench.settings.editor` set to `json` makes Ctrl+, open the user file instead), and below them a tree of the groups (`kit.Tree`, 220 px, resizable) beside the list. A click in the tree scrolls the list to the header of the group (headers are 26 px for a group of the first level, 22 px for the second, 18 px below); scrolling the list selects the group at its top in the tree. "Edit in settings.json" and the button open the file in a text editor (the file is made, as `{}`, when it does not exist) with the cursor on the name of the setting when the file has it, also written as nested objects.
- **Settings in an open editor** (`settings.TextSource`, `gui/app/settingsdocs.go`). VS Code's `JSONEditingService` edits the text model of `settings.json` and saves it only when it was not dirty. The store does the same: while a document is open for a settings file, `Store` computes its edit against the text of the document (`TextSource.OpenText`) and `TextSource.EditText` applies it to the document as one undo step; a document that was not dirty before the edit is saved at once (through the save of Ctrl+S, so the save participants of its language run), a dirty one stays dirty and the file is left alone until the user saves; with no document open the file is written as before. The layer of the store is read from the document too: the window keeps a copy of the text of each document that shows one of its settings files (updated on every change, readable from the goroutine of the file watcher) and reads the settings again when a change leaves the document not dirty (a revert, a reload, an undo to the saved text) and after every edit of the store; typing in a dirty `settings.json` is not applied until it is saved or the store edits it, as VS Code does not apply it either. Every writer of settings goes this way (the settings editor, the Auto Save toggle, the language picker).
- **Colors** (VS Code's names and defaults): `settings.headerForeground`, `settings.settingsHeaderHoverForeground`, `settings.modifiedItemIndicator`, `settings.headerBorder`, `settings.sashBorder`, `settings.dropdownBackground`, `dropdownForeground`, `dropdownBorder`, `dropdownListBorder`, `settings.checkboxBackground`, `checkboxForeground`, `checkboxBorder`, `settings.textInputBackground`, `textInputForeground`, `textInputBorder`, `settings.numberInputBackground`, `numberInputForeground`, `numberInputBorder`, `settings.focusedRowBackground`, `settings.rowHoverBackground` and `settings.focusedRowBorder`. A control is drawn by the kit widget in a `kit.Theme` that gives it these colors as its own. `settings.dropdownListBorder` and `settings.settingsHeaderHoverForeground` are registered and not painted.
- **Not built** (step 8.3 and later, or never): the editors of arrays and objects (they link to the JSON; VS Code has list and object widgets), the settings of extensions, sync, trusted workspaces, policy and tags, the indicators of "Also modified in" as links, the "modified in" scopes of language blocks, the Markdown of descriptions beyond the part above (the links do not open anything, `#setting#` references do not move to the setting), the sticky group header, the narrow layout without the tree, and the settings of a workspace folder that is not the window's. What step 8.2 leaves out is listed after its description.

**Search and the JSON commands as built (step 8.2; `core/settings/{search,defaultjson}.go`, `core/document/virtual.go`, `gui/settingsui`, `gui/app/{settingspane,virtualdocs}.go`).** Ports of `settingsTreeModels.ts` (`parseQuery`, `matchesAnyId`, `matchesAllLanguages`, the `modified` tag), `preferencesSearch.ts` (`cleanFilter`, `SettingMatches`'s key as words, description and enum values) and the open actions of `preferences.contribution.ts`.
- **The query** (`settings.ParseQuery`): filters first, as VS Code's regexes (`(^|\s)@filter:("value"|value)`), then the free text. `@modified` keeps the settings that the file of the tab sets (VS Code's tag `modified`: "configured in the target"; with `@lang:` the block of that language in that file). `@id:a.b,c.d` (a comma separated list, any number of `@id:` filters; case counts; an id ending in `*` is a prefix: `@id:editor.font*`) keeps those whose key is listed. `@lang:go` (an id or a name, whatever the case, as `Registry.ByName` finds it; only the first `@lang:` counts, and with a list the first of it) keeps the settings a language can set (scope `ScopeLanguageOverridable`; VS Code makes the filter act as a scope filter and not as an implicit `@modified`) and shows them as they are in the `[go]` block (below); a language that does not exist finds nothing and a hint says so. `@tag:`, `@feature:`, `@ext:`, `@hasPolicy` and `@stable` are read, taken out of the text and ignored with a hint line ("@tag:x is not supported and was ignored."). Filters combine (all must hold). What is left is the free text, cleaned of `"` and `:` (VS Code's `cleanFilter`) and split into words; a query with no filter and no word is the whole editor (the Commonly Used group included).
- **Matching and ranking** (`UIModel.Search`; simpler than VS Code's match types, which order a flat list): every word must be found, whatever the case, in the key (written as words, `editor.fontSize` is "editor font size", and also with the dots, the case and the separators left out, so `editorfontsize` finds it), in the title ("Editor: Font Size"), in the string values of an enum, or in the description. A word scores its best place: a whole word of the key 4, a part of the key or of the title 3, a value of an enum 2, a whole word of the description 2, a part of it 1; a setting scores the sum of its words, and a query that is the whole key adds 1000. The results are shown **grouped by the groups of the layout, in layout order** (VS Code shows one flat list in the order of its match types), and inside a group in the order of the scores, the layout order among equal scores. Rows keep the titles of the groups they are in ("Font Size" in Font).
- **Result line, hints and tree.** Under the search box (while the query has a filter or a word) the line "N Settings Found" ("1 Setting Found", "No Settings Found", VS Code's `searchResultsCount`), then one line for each hint (an unsupported filter, an unknown language). The list says "No Settings Found." when there is nothing. The tree has only the groups with results, each labeled with the number of the settings found in it and below it ("Font (4)", VS Code's `settings-toc-count`). The query is part of the view state (§10.3) as before; the box has a clear button (the `clear-all` icon, "Clear Search Results") while it has text, and Escape in the box clears it. The search is applied at every change (VS Code waits 200 ms); there is no suggestions dropdown.
- **The settings of a language** (`@lang:`; `UIModel.RowFor`, `SetValueFor`, `SetTextFor`, `ResetFor`; `Store.UpdateForLanguage`, `RemoveForLanguage`). The list gets a title "Language: Go" and the line "The settings below are those of the [go] block of the file."; a row shows the value the `[go]` block of the file of the tab sets, else the value the language's defaults give (the registry's `[go]` defaults), else the default; it is modified (the mark, Reset) when that block of that file sets the setting; "Also modified in" is not shown. Editing writes `"key": value` inside the `"[go]": {…}` block of the file of the tab (the block is made when it is not there; comments and indentation stay, as for any write), and Reset removes the key from the block **and the block itself when it has no other property** (VS Code's `ConfigurationEditing` leaves no empty override block; the comments inside a block that goes with it go too). The Workspace tab edits the block of the workspace file.
- **Commands** (VS Code's ids, titles and categories; all in the palette, none has a key or a menu row beyond those of §11.2): `workbench.action.openSettingsJson` ("Preferences: Open User Settings (JSON)") opens the `settings.json` of the user in a text editor, made as `{` `}` (two lines) when it does not exist; `workbench.action.openWorkspaceSettingsFile` ("Preferences: Open Workspace Settings (JSON)", palette `when workbenchState != 'empty'`) opens `<folder>/.emetos/settings.json` the same way, and without a folder shows "Open a folder to edit the settings of the workspace."; both take `{"revealSetting": {"key": "editor.fontSize"}}` (VS Code's `IOpenSettingsActionOptions`; `edit`, `openToSide`, `focusSearch` and a `query` are read and ignored) and put the cursor on the name of the setting when the file has it (also written as nested objects), else at the start. `workbench.action.openRawDefaultSettings` ("Preferences: Open Default Settings (JSON)") opens the virtual document of §7.1. `workbench.action.openGlobalSettings` ("Preferences: Open User Settings") opens the User tab of the settings editor, as `openSettings` does. `workbench.action.openSettings`, `openSettings2`, `openGlobalSettings` and `openWorkspaceSettings` take the search text as a string or as `{"query": "..."}` (and `{"revealSetting": {"key": k}}` as the query `@id:k`: VS Code scrolls to the setting); with `workbench.settings.editor` set to `json` they open the file. The "Open Settings (JSON)" button of the editor runs `openSettingsJson` (User tab) or `openWorkspaceSettingsFile` (Workspace tab), and "Edit in settings.json" runs the same with `revealSetting`, through the command registry.
- **The default settings document** (`settings.DefaultSettingsJSON(registry)`, `emetos-virtual:/defaultSettings.json`, language `jsonc`, tab "Default Settings"): one JSONC object with every registered setting once, grouped by the groups of the editor in their order (Commonly Used left out), indented with a tab, "\n" lines. A group is announced by a comment with its path ("// Text Editor > Font"); a setting is its description as `//` comments wrapped at 80 columns (Markdown as it is written), then, when they apply, "Allowed values: …" with a line "  value: description" for each described value, "Minimum: n", "Maximum: n" and "Deprecated: …", then `"key": default,` with the default as JSON (objects and arrays indented one level more) and a blank line before the next setting. There is no trailing comma. The default blocks of the languages (`"[go]": {…}`) are not in it.
- **Not built in step 8.2**: the filters `@tag:`, `@feature:`, `@ext:`, `@hasPolicy` and `@stable` (ignored with a hint), the suggestions dropdown of the filters and the filter menu of the search box, the fuzzy and remote (natural language) search and VS Code's match types and TF-IDF ordering, the 200 ms delay of the search, `@id:` without regard to case, "Open Folder Settings" and "Configure Language Specific Settings", the `edit` flag of `revealSetting` (adding the property to the file), the `[language]` blocks of the defaults in the default settings document, the "(Also modified in: …)" notes in the language view, and a target for language blocks other than the tab (the Workspace tab edits the workspace file's block).

### 11.2 Commands and context keys (`core/command`, `core/contextkey`)

- Commands have an id (VS Code's ids where the command exists in VS Code), a title, a category, a precondition, and a handler. The registry holds metadata first; the handler is attached by the package that implements it (an editor command's handler lives in `gui/editor` and calls `core/edit`). Menus, keybindings, the palette and dev commands all run commands by id, with optional JSON-compatible arguments. A command VS Code does not list in the palette (the cursor commands) has no title, and the palette skips it. Setting a handler on a command that has one stacks it: the last one runs, and its remover brings the previous one back. `Run` does not check the precondition; menus, the palette and the dispatcher call `Enabled` first, as VS Code does.
- **Menus** hold items that run commands by id (§10.1 "Menus and the menu bar"). A command that VS Code puts in a menu gets its row in `command/menus_default.go` with VS Code's menu id, group, order, `when` and mnemonic title; the menus show an item dimmed when the precondition of its command does not hold and leave it out when its `when` does not. The built-in menus have rows for the commands that exist, and a menu appears in the menu bar when it has a row.
- Context keys (e.g. `editorTextFocus`, `editorHasSelection`, `findWidgetVisible`, `explorerViewletFocus`, `resourceExtname`) live in scopes: a tree of scopes (window → part → widget); a widget gets a child scope from its parent's and sets its keys there; a lookup walks up the tree; the dispatcher (§5.4) evaluates against the scope of the focused widget. When-clauses use VS Code's syntax: `!`, `&&`, `||`, `==`, `!=`, `=~` (regex), `<`, `>`, `in`, `not in`, parentheses. Evaluation follows JavaScript's rules where VS Code relies on them (`==` is loose, a key that is not set is tested as the string "undefined" by `=~`, `<` and `>` parse the value as a float). Expressions are kept in VS Code's normal form (flat, sorted, `||` outermost) so that `Negate`, `Equals` and `Implies` behave as in VS Code. Regular expressions use Go's engine until `core/regex` has its ECMAScript mode; a pattern it cannot run (lookahead, backreferences) is accepted and never matches.
- The notification host sets `notificationCenterVisible`, `notificationToastsVisible` and `notificationFocus` on the window's keys (step 6.11, §10.7); the Escape rules of `notifications.hideList` and `notifications.hideToasts` have their `when` on them.
- The status bar (step 6.20, §10.1) registers `workbench.action.editor.changeLanguageMode` (Change Language Mode, Ctrl+K M), `workbench.action.editor.changeEOL` (Change End of Line Sequence), `workbench.action.editor.changeEncoding` (Change File Encoding) and `changeEditorIndentation` (no title); the window binds them while the shell is on screen. The setting `files.associations` (an object, default `{}`) is the one the language picker writes. It also registers the colors `statusBarItem.activeBackground`, `hoverBackground`, `hoverForeground`, `compactHoverBackground`, `focusBorder`, `prominentForeground`, `prominentBackground`, `prominentHoverForeground`, `prominentHoverBackground`, `errorBackground`, `errorForeground`, `errorHoverForeground`, `errorHoverBackground`, the same four for `warning`, and `errorForeground`. It sets no context key. The source of a command run by a click on an item is `command.SourceStatusBar`.
- The editor group sets `activeEditor` (the type id of the active input, unset with none), `activeEditorIsPinned` (the active tab is sticky) and `activeEditorIsNotPreview` on the window's keys (step 6.8, §10.3); the Close Editor row of the File menu has the precondition `activeEditor`. The grid of groups (step 6.9) adds `multipleEditorGroups`, `editorPartMultipleEditorGroups`, `editorPartMaximizedEditorGroup` and `activeEditorGroupEmpty`; the Switch Group rows and `toggleMaximizeEditorGroup` have their preconditions on them and Ctrl+W has a second rule, `closeGroup`, under `activeEditorGroupEmpty && multipleEditorGroups`.

- Saving (step 7.1, §6.3) registers `workbench.action.files.save` (Save, Ctrl+S), `workbench.action.files.saveAs` (Save As..., Ctrl+Shift+S), `workbench.action.files.saveWithoutFormatting` (Save without Formatting, Ctrl+K S on Linux; it skips every save participant, since there is no formatter), `saveAll` (VS Code's id of Save All; no key on Linux: VS Code gives Ctrl+K S to it on Windows only) and `workbench.action.files.saveAll` (Save All in the palette, which runs it), `workbench.action.files.revert` (Revert File) and `workbench.action.files.newUntitledFile` (New Untitled Text File, Ctrl+N; its argument may be `{"languageId": "go"}`). The File menu has New Text File (`1_new`), Save, Save As... and Save All (`4_save`; enabled by `activeEditor` and `dirtyWorkingCopies`) and Revert File (`6_close`, `activeEditorCanRevert`). Settings: `files.trimTrailingWhitespace`, `files.insertFinalNewline`, `files.trimFinalNewlines`, `files.defaultLanguage` and `files.saveConflictResolution` (`files.encoding` and `files.eol` were registered before). Context keys: `activeEditorIsDirty`, `activeEditorCanRevert`, `dirtyWorkingCopies`. The commands act on the tab their argument names (the menus of a tab) or on the active editor.
- Closing (step 7.2, §10.3, §10.10, §10.11) registers `workbench.action.closeWindow` (Close Window, in the palette; Alt+F4 and Ctrl+Shift+W on Linux; File menu row "Clos&&e Window", group `6_close`, order 4) and `workbench.action.quit` (no title, so no palette entry, as VS Code; Ctrl+Q; File menu row "E&&xit" titled "Exit", group `z_Exit`, order 1), both bound by the shell to `Window.RequestClose`; and the setting `files.hotExit` (§10.10). The existing close commands ask about unsaved changes. No context key is added.
- Auto save (step 7.3, §6.3) registers `workbench.action.toggleAutoSave` (Toggle Auto Save, category File, in the palette, no key; File menu row "A&&uto Save", group `5_autosave`, order 1, toggled by `config.files.autoSave != 'off'`, the `Toggled` condition of a menu row, §10.1) and the settings `files.autoSave`, `files.autoSaveDelay`, `files.autoSaveWorkspaceFilesOnly` and `files.autoSaveWhenNoErrors`. No context key is added. The settings are context keys of the form `config.<setting>` in every `when` the window evaluates: the menus (`menuContext`) and, since this step, the key dispatcher (`Window.DispatchKey` evaluates a keybinding's `when` in the scope of the focused widget over the settings), as VS Code's configuration keys; a key of the scope wins over a setting of the same name.
- Restore and recent lists (step 7.5, §10.10) register `workbench.action.openRecent` (Open Recent..., category File, in the palette; Ctrl+R on Linux, VS Code's key; File menu row "&&More..." in the Open Recent submenu, group `y_more`), `workbench.action.clearRecentFiles` (Clear Recently Opened..., category File, in the palette, no key; row "&&Clear Recently Opened..." in the submenu, group `z_clear`), and `openRecentFolder` and `openRecentFile` (no title, so not in the palette; the rows of the recent folders and files run them with the path as the argument); the File menu has the submenu row "Open &&Recent" (group `2_open`, order 4, VS Code's) whose rows are the dynamic ones of `MenuRegistry.SetProvider` plus Reopen Closed Editor (group `1_editor`, order 1). The settings are `window.restoreWindows` (`preserve`, `all`, `folders`, `one`, `none`; default `all`; application scope) and `window.restoreFullscreen` (default false; application scope). No context key is added.
- Zoom, full screen, zen mode and the centered layout (step 8.5, §10.9) register `workbench.action.zoomIn` (Zoom In, Ctrl+=, Ctrl+Shift+=, Ctrl+NumPad_Add), `zoomOut` (Zoom Out, Ctrl+-, Ctrl+NumPad_Subtract), `zoomReset` (Reset Zoom, Ctrl+NumPad0), `toggleFullScreen` (Toggle Full Screen, F11), `toggleZenMode` (Toggle Zen Mode, Ctrl+K Z), `exitZenMode` (no title; Escape Escape when `inZenMode`) and `toggleCenteredLayout` (Toggle Centered Layout, no key), all with the category View but `exitZenMode`; the View > Appearance rows of §10.9; the settings `window.zoomLevel`, `window.zoomPerWindow`, `zenMode.fullScreen`, `zenMode.centerLayout`, `zenMode.showTabs`, `zenMode.hideStatusBar`, `zenMode.hideActivityBar`, `zenMode.hideLineNumbers`, `zenMode.restore`, `zenMode.silentNotifications`, `workbench.editor.centeredLayoutAutoResize` and `workbench.editor.centeredLayoutFixedWidth` (VS Code's defaults and texts; `zenMode.hideTabs` is not registered). The window sets the context keys `isFullscreen`, `inZenMode` and `isCenteredLayout`. The handlers are the window's (`gui/app/zoom.go`, `zen.go`).
- Windows (step 7.8, §10.11) register `workbench.action.newWindow` (New Window, in the palette, Ctrl+Shift+N, File menu row "New &&Window", group `1_new`, order 3) and `workbench.action.switchWindow` (Switch Window..., in the palette, no key), and the settings `window.openFoldersInNewWindow` (`on`, `off`, `default`; default `default`), `window.openFilesInNewWindow` (`on`, `off`, `default`; default `off`) and `window.newWindowDimensions` (`default`, `inherit`, `offset`, `maximized`, `fullscreen`; default `default`), all of application scope, with VS Code's texts. The window has its own registry of commands (§5.1); the commands about windows are bound when the window is made. No context key is added.
- External changes (step 7.4, §6.3) register no command and no key, and set no context key. They register the setting `workbench.editor.decorations.colors` (boolean, true, window scope, VS Code's description "Controls whether editor file decorations should use colors.") and the color `list.errorForeground` (§10.3); `workbench.editor.closeOnFileDelete` (step 6.15) is used as before. The notification buttons (Overwrite, Revert) are notification actions, not commands.
- The panels set `activePanel`, `problemsVisibility`, `focusedView`, `problemFocus`, `problemsFilterFocus`, `problems.filter.errors`, `problems.filter.warnings`, `problems.filter.info`, `problems.filter.activeFile`, `inOutput`, `outputView.scrollLock` and `activeOutputChannel` on the window's keys (step 6.12, §10.13); the Ctrl+F, Ctrl+Down and Escape rules of the Problems view have their `when` on `focusedView` and `problemsFilterFocus`.
- The quick input sets `inQuickOpen`, `inQuickInput`, `inputFocus` and `quickInputType` (`"quickPick"`) on the window's keys while it is open, and the key of the mode that is active (`inCommandsPicker` for `>`, `inViewsPicker` for `view `; a provider names it in `ProviderInfo.ContextKey`) (step 6.17, §10.6). Its Escape rules have their `when` on `inQuickOpen`. The `CommandPalette` menu has no row that shows: a row of it whose `when` does not hold keeps its command out of the palette (VS Code's `f1: false`), and the palette also leaves out the commands that need an editor while none is open.
- The file mode of the quick input sets `inFilesPicker` (step 6.18, §10.6) while it is the active mode, as VS Code's `defaultQuickAccessContextKeyValue`; no keybinding has it in its `when`.
- The editor modes of the quick input set `inEditorsPicker` while one is active (step 6.19, §10.6); the keys that move in the switcher (`quickOpenNavigateNextInEditorPicker`, `quickOpenNavigatePreviousInEditorPicker`) have `inQuickOpen && inEditorsPicker` as their `when`. The history sets `canNavigateBack`, `canNavigateForward`, `canNavigateBackInNavigationLocations`, `canNavigateForwardInNavigationLocations`, `canNavigateToLastNavigationLocation`, `canNavigateBackInEditLocations`, `canNavigateForwardInEditLocations`, `canNavigateToLastEditLocation` and `canReopenClosedEditor` (step 6.19, §10.3); Go Back and Go Forward have the first two in their `when` and their menu rows are dimmed by them, and the Ctrl+Tab keys have `!activeEditorGroupEmpty`.
- The settings editor (step 8.1, §10.3, §11.1) registers `workbench.action.openSettings` (title "Settings", Ctrl+, with no `when`; rows "&&Settings" in the Preferences submenu of the File menu, group `2_configuration`, order 2, and in the Manage menu; the palette leaves it out, as VS Code does), `workbench.action.openSettings2` ("Preferences: Open Settings (UI)", the palette's entry; it runs the same) and `workbench.action.openWorkspaceSettings` ("Preferences: Open Workspace Settings", in the palette while `workbenchState != 'empty'`; it opens the Workspace tab). The File menu has the submenu row "&&Preferences" (group `5_autosave`, order 2). The argument of the first two is the text of the search (a string or `{"query": "..."}`); an open settings editor is revealed (VS Code's target of `openSettings` is the user's, so the tab goes back to User) and its search box gets the focus. The setting `workbench.settings.editor` (`ui` or `json`, default `ui`; window scope) says which editor Ctrl+, opens. The colors are those of §11.1. No context key is added.
- The JSON commands of the settings (step 8.2, §7.1, §11.1) register `workbench.action.openSettingsJson` ("Open User Settings (JSON)"), `workbench.action.openWorkspaceSettingsFile` ("Open Workspace Settings (JSON)"; palette `when workbenchState != 'empty'`), `workbench.action.openRawDefaultSettings` ("Open Default Settings (JSON)") and `workbench.action.openGlobalSettings` ("Open User Settings"), all in the category Preferences and in the palette (as VS Code's `f1`); none has a key or a menu row (the File > Preferences menu has Settings only, as VS Code's). `openSettingsJson` and `openWorkspaceSettingsFile` take `{"revealSetting": {"key": "..."}}`; `openSettings`, `openSettings2` and `openGlobalSettings` also take the same object as the search `@id:<key>`. No setting or context key is added.
- The keybindings editor (step 8.3, §11.3) registers `workbench.action.openGlobalKeybindings` ("Open Keyboard Shortcuts", category Preferences, Ctrl+K Ctrl+S; row "&&Keyboard Shortcuts" in the Preferences submenu of the File menu and in the Manage menu, group `2_configuration`, order 4), `workbench.action.openGlobalKeybindingsFile` ("Open Keyboard Shortcuts (JSON)") and `workbench.action.openDefaultKeybindingsFile` ("Open Default Keyboard Shortcuts (JSON)"), all in the palette. The first takes the text of the search (a string or `{"query": "..."}`). The commands of the editor, `keybindings.editor.searchKeybindings` (Ctrl+F), `clearSearchResults` ("Clear Search Results", Escape; the only one with a title, and the palette leaves it out), `recordSearchKeys` (Alt+K), `focusKeybindings` (Ctrl+Down), `defineKeybinding` (Enter), `addKeybinding` (Ctrl+K Ctrl+A), `defineWhenExpression` (Ctrl+K Ctrl+E), `removeKeybinding` (Delete), `resetKeybinding`, `showConflicts`, `copyKeybindingEntry` (Ctrl+C), `copyCommandKeybindingEntry` and `copyCommandTitle` have no title (they are not in the palette), all with the weight of the workbench contributions. The editor sets the context keys `inKeybindings` (the focus is in the editor), `inKeybindingsSearch` (in the search box), `keybindingsSearchHasValue` (the search box has text), `keybindingFocus` (a row of the table has the focus), `whenFocus` (the box of the when-clause of a row has the focus) and `inputFocus` (any text box of the editor has the focus). The colors are `tree.tableColumnsBorder`, `tree.tableOddRowsBackground`, `keybindingTable.headerBackground` and `keybindingTable.rowsBackground`. No setting is added.
- The theme pickers (step 8.4, §8.4, §10.8) register `workbench.action.selectTheme` ("Color Theme", category Preferences, Ctrl+K Ctrl+T, weight of the workbench contributions) and `workbench.action.selectIconTheme` ("File Icon Theme", category Preferences, no key); both are in the palette ("Preferences: Color Theme", "Preferences: File Icon Theme"). The menu `ThemesSubMenu` has the rows "Color Theme" (order 1) and "File Icon Theme" (order 2), and is a submenu row "&&Themes" in the Preferences submenu of the File menu and "Themes" in the Manage menu, both in the group `2_configuration`, order 7 (after Settings and Keyboard Shortcuts). VS Code's third row, "Product Icon Theme", and its other theme commands are not registered. No context key and no setting is added: `workbench.colorTheme` (default "Dark Modern") and `workbench.iconTheme` (`string` or `null`, default `vs-seti`) were registered before; `window.autoDetectColorScheme`, `workbench.preferredDarkColorTheme` and `workbench.preferredLightColorTheme` act as in §8.4, and their UI (the button of the picker that opens them) is not built.
- The profile commands (step 8.6b, §10.12) register `workbench.profiles.actions.createProfile` ("Create Profile..."), `workbench.profiles.actions.switchProfile` ("Switch Profile..."), `workbench.profiles.actions.renameProfile` ("Rename Profile..."), `workbench.profiles.actions.deleteProfile` ("Delete Profile..."), `workbench.profiles.actions.exportProfile` ("Export Profile...") and `workbench.profiles.actions.importProfile` ("Import Profile..."), all in the category Profiles ("Profiles: Switch Profile...") and in the palette, with no key. The menu `ProfilesSubMenu` has their rows and is a submenu row "&&Profiles" in the Preferences submenu of the File menu and "Profiles" in the Manage menu, group `2_configuration`, order 1. The status bar item `status.profile` runs `switchProfile`. No context key and no setting is added.
- The window and the Explorer set `workbenchState` (`"folder"` or `"empty"`), `explorerViewletVisible`, `foldersViewVisible`, `explorerViewletFocus`, `filesExplorerFocus`, `openEditorsFocus`, `explorerResourceIsFolder`, `explorerResourceIsRoot`, `explorerResourceReadonly` and `groupFocusedInOpenEditors` (step 6.14, §10.8); the Close Folder row of the File menu and the views of the Explorer have their `when` on `workbenchState`.

### 11.3 Keybindings (`core/keybinding`)

- The default keymap is VS Code's Linux keymap for the commands Emetos has. VS Code registers its defaults in code (`registerKeybinding`/`KeybindingsRegistry` calls in `coreCommands.ts` and the contributions); each step that adds a command adds its default binding from there.
- `keybindings.json` entries: `key` (with chords such as `ctrl+k ctrl+c`), `command` (a leading `-` removes a default), `when`, `args`. A leading `^` in a default's command id means the key is not consumed. An entry whose key, command id or when-clause cannot be read is reported as a problem and skipped (VS Code keeps an entry with an unknown key as an unbound command, so a typo in a removal would remove every key of the command). The `[KeyCode]` spelling of keys is not supported.
- Keys are VS Code's `KeyCode`s; the resolver matches the string `ctrl+shift+alt+meta+<Key>` of a pressed chord (the UI name of the key, such as `K`, `LeftArrow`, `[`). A pressed sequence resolves to no match, more chords needed, or a command; of several bindings that match, the last one registered whose when-clause holds wins, after the sort by weight (core 0, contributions 100, workbench 200), command id and secondary-key order. The dispatcher (`keybinding.Dispatcher`) keeps the pending chords and forgets them after 5 seconds (`Expire`, called from a timer); a key that completes no binding ends chord mode and is consumed, and shows "The key combination (Ctrl+K, X) is not a command.". Keybindings made of a lone modifier ("shift shift") resolve, but the dispatcher does not yet detect the double press (it needs key-up events: the workbench step).
- A chord's first key shows "(Ctrl+K) was pressed. Waiting for second key of chord..." in the status bar (step 6.20: `Window.ShowStatusMessage`, the last item of the left side). The message goes when the chord is completed or aborted, and when the dispatcher forgets the chord after `ChordTimeout` (a timer of the window calls `LeaveChordMode`); the message of a key that is not a command stays 10 seconds, as in VS Code.
- A **recording mode** (`Dispatcher.SetRecorder`) hands every key press to a `Recorder` as a `Chord` and consumes it; nothing is resolved while a recorder is set, and the pending chords are forgotten. The keybindings editor uses it for the search box and for the box that defines a key.
- `Resolver.Replace` swaps the contents of a resolver in place, so that the menus, the status bar and the dispatcher, which hold the pointer, follow a change of `keybindings.json` at once. `Item.FileEntry` is the 1-based position of an item in `keybindings.json` (0 for a default); `UserKeybindingsPath` is `<config dir>/keybindings.json`.

**The keybindings editor as built (step 8.3; `core/keybinding/{model,search,conflicts,editing,defaultjson}.go`, `core/editorgroup/keybindingsinput.go`, `gui/keybindingsui`, `gui/kit/keycaps.go`, `gui/app/{keybindingspane,userkeys}.go`).** Ports of `keybindingsEditor.ts`, `keybindingsEditorModel.ts`, `keybindingsEditorContribution.ts`, `keybindingEditing.ts` and `defineKeybindingWidget.ts`. The commands, keys, context keys and colors are in §11.2.
- **The model** (`keybinding.Model`, `NewModel(defaults, user, commands)`). One row (`Row`) for each default or user keybinding of a command, after the removals (`-command` entries) are applied, and one row without a key for each command that has none (listed once). A row has the command id, its label (the title, with the category in front: "Preferences: Open Keyboard Shortcuts"), the keybinding, the `when` text, the source (`Default` or `User`; a command that lost its default key shows its unbound row as `User`) and the position in the file (`FileEntry`). Commands whose id starts with `_` or `vscode.` and the `dev.` commands are left out. Rows are sorted as VS Code sorts them by name: keys first, then labelled commands, by label (`fuzzy.Collate`) and command id, user rows before default rows of the same command. Sorting by precedence is not built.
- **Search** (`Model.Fetch(text)`, a list of `Result`s with the ranges that matched in the label, id, when and source and the keys that matched). As VS Code's `KeybindingItemMatches`: the words of the text match the label (words, camel case or contiguous), the command id, the when-clause and the source; a text in quotes (`"ctrl+k"`) matches the keybinding exactly (the first chord, the whole sequence of a longer one); a word that is a key name (or modifier) matches the keys that have it. The filters are `@source:user`, `@source:default` (`system` is accepted as `default`), `@command:<id>` (with `+when:<clause>`) and `@keybinding:<keys>`; the filters of extensions are not built. The matches are drawn in the highlight color of lists, in the label, in the id, in the when text and on the key caps.
- **The page** (`keybindingsui.Editor`). A search box ("Type to search in keybindings") with two buttons, record keys (Alt+K: the box then shows the badge "Recording Keys" and the placeholder "Recording Keys. Press Escape to exit", and every press becomes the quoted text of the search, `"ctrl+shift+p"`, up to two chords; Escape ends it) and clear (Escape clears a search); a table with the columns Command, Keybinding, When and Source, a header, zebra rows, and the rows as a `kit.List` with its own heights (a row that matched by id shows the id on a second line). The keybinding is drawn as key caps (`kit.NewKeyCaps`, shared with the quick input). A hovered, selected or focused row shows the pencil (Change Keybinding) or, for a row without a key, the plus (Add Keybinding). A double click on a row (or Enter) defines its key; the secondary button opens the context menu: Copy, Copy Command ID, Copy Command Title, Change Keybinding..., Add Keybinding..., Remove Keybinding, Reset Keybinding (only for a row from the user's file), Change When Expression, Show Same Keybindings (the search becomes `"<keys>"`). The button "Open Keyboard Shortcuts (JSON)" in the header opens `keybindings.json`. The view state (the search and the selected row) is kept with the input and comes back with the state of the window; the recording of keys is not kept.
- **Defining a key** (`define.go`). Enter or the pencil opens a box over the table: "Press desired key combination and then press ENTER.", the keys as text and as key caps, and the number of commands that already have the keys ("1 existing command has this keybinding", a click on it shows them in the table; at most four are listed). The dispatcher is in recording mode: every press is recorded (two chords at most, a lone modifier is an unfinished chord that the next press replaces; Backspace is a key, as in VS Code). Enter confirms, Escape clears the recorded keys and, with none, closes the box; a click outside closes it. Choosing the keys of the row it already has changes nothing. With conflicts (below) a dialog asks what to do.
- **Conflicts** (`Model.FindConflicts(keybinding, when, command)`). The rows of other commands with exactly the same sequence of chords, whose when-clauses can both hold: two clauses overlap unless one provably excludes the other (one implies the negation of the other, with `contextkey.Implies`); empty or equal clauses overlap, and clauses that cannot be compared overlap. VS Code lists every row with the same keys; keys that are a prefix of a longer sequence are not reported. The dialog "Keybinding conflicts" has the buttons Keep Both (the default), Overwrite and Cancel. Keep Both adds the entry. Overwrite adds it and removes the others: an entry of the user's file with those keys is deleted, a default one gets a `-command` entry. Cancel does nothing.
- **Editing `keybindings.json`** (`keybinding.Editing`, each operation takes the text and gives the new text; the editor applies it as a minimal text edit, so comments and layout stay). Entries are written with the keys `key`, `command`, `when`, `args` in this order, indented as the file is (else by `editor.tabSize` and `editor.insertSpaces`); a file with comments only gets the array appended, an empty file or a missing one becomes `// Place your key bindings in this file to override the defaults` and `[]`. A file with syntax errors is not edited (`KeybindingsEditFileInvalid`), nor one whose root is not an array (`KeybindingsEditNotArray`).
  - Add (`Add`): appends `{ "key", "command", "when", "args" }`; the `when` and `args` are those of the row (the arguments of a default are carried to the new entry).
  - Change (`Edit`, a row with a key): a row from the user's file is changed in place (its `key`, found by `FileEntry` and checked against the command); a default row gets a new entry and the removal `{ "key": "<old key>", "command": "-<command>", "when": "<old when>" }` (not repeated when it is there).
  - Change the when-clause (`UpdateWhen`): the same, for the `when`; the clause is checked first (`ValidateWhen`: "Not a when-clause: ..." is shown under the box and nothing is written). An empty text removes the `when`.
  - Remove (`Remove`): a row from the user's file loses its entry; a default one gets the removal entry.
  - Reset (`Reset`): every entry of the command in the user's file, its removals included, is deleted, so the defaults come back.
  - Errors: `KeybindingsEditFailed` (a text edit of `core/jsonc` failed), `KeybindingEditKeyMissing`, `KeybindingEditCommandMissing`, `KeybindingEditUnbound` (a row without a key cannot be changed or removed), `KeybindingEntryNotFound` (the entry of the row is not in the file any more), `KeybindingsFileWriteFailed`, `KeybindingsFileCreateFailed` and `KeybindingsInputInvalid`. A failed edit is logged and shown as an error notification.
- **The file in the window** (`userKeys`). `keybindings.json` is read when the window opens and again when it changes (the watcher, a document of it that is saved, an edit of the editor); the resolver of the window is replaced in place (`Resolver.Replace`) and any chord in progress is dropped, so the keys, the menus and the status bar follow at once. An entry that cannot be read is skipped and logged (§11.3 above).
